		\item Gradient \textbf{w.r.t.\ the input}, not the weights -- weights are fixed. The \textbf{regularisation} term keeps pixels in natural ranges (avoids extreme values).
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Data Augmentation}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		Artificially generate more training data by applying random \textbf{label-preserving} transformations. Goal: the model rarely sees the exact same image twice $\rightarrow$ less overfitting, better generalisation (a form of regularisation).
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Geometric}: rotation, translation (width/height shift), flip, shear, zoom/crop.
		\item \textbf{Photometric}: colour shift, illumination, contrast, noise.
		\item \textbf{Caveat}: must preserve the label -- e.g.\ horizontal flip breaks text/digits (a flipped STOP sign or ``5'').
		\item \textbf{Offline (pre-)}: build an enlarged dataset once. \textbf{Online}: transform each batch on the fly (Keras \texttt{ImageDataGenerator} $\rightarrow$ \texttt{flow}); produces ``infinite'' batches, so set \texttt{steps\_per\_epoch} $=$ \#images$/$batch.
		\item Modern alternative: \textbf{preprocessing layers} (\texttt{RandomRotation}, \texttt{RandomFlip}\dots) inside the model $\rightarrow$ runs in the graph / on GPU.
		\item Effect: a model trained \emph{with} augmentation stays robust on both clean and augmented test data; a model trained \emph{without} drops sharply on augmented data ($\sim$91\%$\to$78\%).
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Conv-Layer Pattern \& Deep Architectures}

	\begin{importantbox}
		Common CNN macro-pattern (compact notation, \emph{not} a language):
		\[
		\texttt{INPUT} \to [[\texttt{CONV}\to\texttt{RELU}]{*}N \to \texttt{POOL?}]{*}M \to [\texttt{FC}\to\texttt{RELU}]{*}K \to \texttt{FC}
		\]
		$N{\le}3$, $K{<}3$. Stacking \textbf{2+ CONV before each POOL} builds richer features before the ``destructive'' downsampling.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{center}\footnotesize
	\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{6.3cm}@{}}
		\toprule
		Model & Year & Key idea \\
		\midrule
		LeNet-5  & 1998 & First successful CNN (digits/zip codes). \texttt{[CONV-POOL-CONV-POOL-FC-FC]}, $5{\times}5$ convs. \\
		AlexNet  & 2012 & ILSVRC breakout (16\% vs 26\%). Deeper, ReLU (first use), dropout, heavy augmentation, 2-GPU, \textbf{$\sim$60M} params. \\
		ZFNet    & 2013 & AlexNet with better hyper-parameters. \\
		VGGNet   & 2014 & \textbf{Only $3{\times}3$ CONV} ($S{=}1,P{=}1$) + $2{\times}2$ POOL; 16--19 layers. Most memory in CONV, most params in FC ($\sim$138M). \\
		GoogLeNet& 2014 & \textbf{Inception} module + $1{\times}1$ bottlenecks; \textbf{no FC}; only \textbf{$\sim$5M} params (12$\times$ less than AlexNet); 22 layers. \\
		ResNet   & 2015 & \textbf{Residual blocks} $\rightarrow$ 152 layers; ILSVRC'15 winner (3.6\%). BN after every conv, global avg pool. \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		\textbf{Worked AlexNet CONV1}: input $227{\times}227{\times}3$, $96$ filters $11{\times}11$, $S{=}4$, $P{=}0$.
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
			\item Output: $O=\frac{227-11}{4}+1=55 \Rightarrow 55{\times}55{\times}96$.
			\item Params: $96{\cdot}(11{\cdot}11{\cdot}3)+96 = \mathbf{34\,944}\approx35\text{K}$.
			\item Then POOL1 $3{\times}3,S{=}2$: $O=\frac{55-3}{2}+1=27 \Rightarrow 27{\times}27{\times}96$, \textbf{0} params.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{VGG: Small Filters, Deeper}

	\begin{importantbox}
		A stack of \textbf{three $3{\times}3$} convs ($S{=}1$) has the \textbf{same receptive field as one $7{\times}7$} conv, but with \textbf{fewer parameters} ($3{\cdot}3C^2$ vs $7{\cdot}7C^2$) and \textbf{more non-linearities} (3 ReLUs vs 1). This is why small-filter deep stacks beat large-filter shallow ones.
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{GoogLeNet: Inception Module}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Idea}: don't pick one filter size -- run $1{\times}1$, $3{\times}3$, $5{\times}5$ convs and a $3{\times}3$ pool \textbf{in parallel}, then \textbf{depth-concatenate} the outputs (``network in network'', multi-scale receptive fields).
		\item \textbf{$1{\times}1$ bottleneck} convs reduce depth \emph{before} the expensive $3{\times}3$/$5{\times}5$ $\rightarrow$ far fewer operations (preserve spatial size, project channels).
		\item \textbf{Auxiliary classifiers} at intermediate layers inject extra gradient into early layers (helps training the deep stack). \textbf{No FC} layers $\rightarrow$ tiny parameter count.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{ResNet: Residual Connections}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Degradation problem}: a deeper plain net (56 layers) had \emph{higher} train \emph{and} test error than a shallower one (20 layers) -- \textbf{not} overfitting, but an \textbf{optimisation} difficulty.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{formulabox}
		Fit a \textbf{residual} instead of the full mapping:
		\[
		y = \mathcal{F}(x,\{W_i\}) + x, \qquad \mathcal{F}(x) = H(x) - x
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=8mm, >=Stealth,
		b/.style={draw, fill=orange!18, minimum width=20mm, minimum height=6mm, font=\scriptsize}]
		\node (x) {$x$};
		\node[b, above=of x] (c1) {$3{\times}3$ conv};
		\node[b, above=of c1] (c2) {$3{\times}3$ conv};
		\node[draw, circle, above=of c2, inner sep=1pt] (p) {$+$};
		\node[above=6mm of p, font=\scriptsize] (y) {ReLU $\to y$};
		\draw[->](x)--(c1); \draw[->](c1)-- node[right,font=\scriptsize]{ReLU}(c2); \draw[->](c2)--(p); \draw[->](p)--(y);
		\draw[->] (x.east) -- ++(16mm,0) |- node[right, pos=0.25, font=\scriptsize]{identity $x$} (p.east);
	\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item The \textbf{skip/identity connection} lets gradients flow directly $\rightarrow$ trains 100+ layers without vanishing gradients; if a block isn't useful it can \textbf{fall back to identity} ($\mathcal{F}\to0$).
		\item \textbf{Bottleneck} ($1{\times}1\to3{\times}3\to1{\times}1$) to go beyond 50 layers; BN after every conv; global average pooling $\rightarrow$ single FC; no dropout.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Exam Tips}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Likely questions:
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
			\item \emph{Compute output size / \#params} through a conv/pool stack ($O=\frac{W-w+2P}{S}+1$, $N=whCD+D$); pooling \& activations add \textbf{0} params; FC layers dominate.
			\item \emph{Why three $3{\times}3$ instead of one $7{\times}7$?} -- same receptive field, fewer params, more non-linearity (VGG).
			\item \emph{What is an Inception module / a $1{\times}1$ conv for?} -- parallel multi-scale filters + depth concat; $1{\times}1$ = channel bottleneck to cut compute.
			\item \emph{What problem does ResNet solve and how?} -- degradation (optimisation, not overfitting); residual $y=\mathcal{F}(x)+x$ with identity skip.
			\item \emph{Why data augmentation?} -- fights overfitting by label-preserving transforms; name online vs offline.
			\item \emph{How are CNN features visualised?} -- activation maps; gradient-ascent on the input to maximise a neuron.
		\end{itemize}
		\textbf{Common mistakes:} forgetting the bias/$+1$ in counts; saying the deeper-net failure in ResNet is overfitting (it's an optimisation/degradation problem); augmenting in a way that breaks the label (flipping text); confusing \emph{which} layers hold most params (FC) vs most memory/compute (CONV).
	\end{examtipbox}
	% ==================================================
	\section{Training Deep Networks: Vanishing Gradients}
	% ==================================================

	\subsection{The Vanishing / Exploding Gradient Problem}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		In deep nets the backprop gradient is a \textbf{product} of per-layer Jacobians. If the factors are $<1$ it \textbf{vanishes} (exponentially with depth) $\rightarrow$ early layers barely learn; if $>1$ it \textbf{explodes} $\rightarrow$ unstable. Glorot \& Bengio (2010): with sigmoid + naive init, gradients shrink in earlier layers.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{formulabox}
		Per-layer backward step (from backprop):
		\[
		\frac{\partial L}{\partial a^{[l-1]}} = \frac{\partial L}{\partial a^{[l]}}\odot \underbrace{\sigma'(z^{[l]})}_{\text{small if saturated}}\cdot\, \underbrace{W^{[l]}}_{\text{ill-conditioned}\Rightarrow\,\text{vanish/explode}}
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{importantbox}
		The cure: keep the distribution of the logits $z^{[l]}=a^{[l-1]}(W^{[l]})^{\top}+b^{[l]}$ in the activation's \textbf{dynamic range}, in \emph{both} forward and backward passes. Three levers: \textbf{activation function}, \textbf{normalisation} (input + batch), \textbf{weight initialisation}.
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Activation Functions}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Non-linearity is essential}: without it the whole net collapses to one linear layer (no representational capacity). Exception: the \emph{output} layer of a regression task (linear).
		\item \textbf{Saturating / S-shaped} (sigmoid, tanh, softsign, step): for large $|z|$ the output flattens $\rightarrow$ $\sigma'(z)\approx0$ $\rightarrow$ \textbf{vanishing gradients}. A wider logit distribution hits saturation more often.
		\item \textbf{ReLU} $\max(0,z)$: no saturation for $z>0$, but \textbf{dying units} -- for $z<0$ both activation \emph{and} derivative are $0$ $\rightarrow$ neuron never updates, stays ``dead''.
		\item \textbf{Leaky ReLU} $\max(\eta z, z)$ ($\eta\approx0.01$) / \textbf{ELU}: non-zero gradient for $z<0$ $\rightarrow$ no dying units.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}\footnotesize
	\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
		\toprule
		Function & Formula & Note \\
		\midrule
		Sigmoid    & $1/(1+e^{-z})$ & saturates; binary-class output \\
		Tanh       & $\tanh z$ & saturates; zero-centred \\
		ReLU       & $\max(0,z)$ & dying units \\
		Leaky ReLU & $\max(\eta z,z)$ & fixes dying units \\
		GeLU       & $z\,\Phi(z)$ & $\Phi$ = Gaussian CDF; transformers \\
		SiLU/Swish & $z\,\sigma(z)$ & modern vision models \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Last-layer by task}: binary $\rightarrow$ sigmoid; multiclass $\rightarrow$ softmax; regression $\rightarrow$ none (linear).
		\item Default hidden choices: ReLU/Leaky\,ReLU (classical), GeLU (transformers), SiLU (modern vision).
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Input Normalisation}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		Make input features \textbf{centred} (zero mean) and on \textbf{similar scales} so logits land in the activation's dynamic range. Stats are computed on the \textbf{training set only} (then reused for val/test).
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{z-normalisation / standardisation} (per feature $k$):
		\[
		x'^{(i)}_k = \frac{x^{(i)}_k - \mu_k}{\sigma_k}, \quad
		\mu_k=\tfrac1N\textstyle\sum_i x_k^{(i)},\;\;
		\sigma_k^2=\tfrac1N\textstyle\sum_i (x_k^{(i)}-\mu_k)^2
		\]
		\textbf{Min-max} $\rightarrow[0,1]$: $\dfrac{x-\min_k}{\max_k-\min_k}$; \quad centred $\rightarrow[-1,1]$: $2\cdot\dfrac{x-\min_k}{\max_k-\min_k}-1$.
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Why}: removes arbitrary units; lets weight-init rules be \emph{data-independent}; learning starts on the patterns, not on correcting scale/offset. Large features $\Rightarrow$ large $\Delta W$ but small $W$ needed ($xW\!\sim\!1$) -- a conflict normalisation removes.
		\item \textbf{Standardisation} for Gaussian-like / unbounded features; \textbf{min-max} when natural bounds exist. Images: stats over all pixels (not per pixel). PyTorch: \texttt{transforms.Normalize(mean,std)}.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Batch Normalisation}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{BatchNorm} (Ioffe \& Szegedy 2015): normalise the logits $Z$ of a layer using the \textbf{mini-batch} mean/std (per feature), then apply a \textbf{learnable} scale $\gamma$ and shift $\beta$, then the activation. Applicable to any layer.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{formulabox}
		Per feature $k$, over batch of size $N_B$:
		\[
		\hat Z = \frac{Z-\mu_k}{\sqrt{\sigma_k^2+\epsilon}}, \qquad \overline Z = \gamma_k \hat Z + \beta_k
		\]
		\textbf{Test/production} (no batch): use running averages, $\hat\mu^{(r+1)}=(1-\nu)\hat\mu^{(r)}+\nu\mu^{(r)}$ (same for $\sigma$).
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item The bias $b$ is redundant (absorbed by $\beta$). Place BN \textbf{before the non-linearity}: \texttt{CONV/FC $\to$ BN $\to$ activation ($\to$ Dropout)}.
		\item \textbf{Effects}: much faster training (Inception: $\sim$2--14$\times$ fewer steps), allows higher learning rates, mild regularisation, even rescues sigmoid nets.
		\item \textbf{Why it works}: original claim = reduce ``internal covariate shift''; later work (Santurkar 2018) argues it mainly \textbf{smooths the loss surface} / decouples layers $\rightarrow$ more robust training.
		\item \textbf{Train vs test differ} $\rightarrow$ must call \texttt{model.train()} / \texttt{model.eval()}. Needs a \textbf{large enough batch}; small batches $\rightarrow$ use \textbf{LayerNorm} (per sample, same at train/test).
		\item Variants: \textbf{BatchNorm} (over N,H,W per channel), \textbf{LayerNorm} (over C,H,W per sample), \textbf{InstanceNorm} (over H,W per channel per sample), GroupNorm.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Parameter Initialisation}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Random} init breaks symmetry (else all neurons learn the same). \textbf{Scale} the variance so logit/gradient variance is preserved across layers (no early saturation). Choose the scheme by the \textbf{activation}.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{formulabox}
		Forward stability wants $\mathrm{Var}(W)=\dfrac{1}{c\,N_{l-1}}$ (fan-in), backward wants $\dfrac{1}{c\,N_l}$ (fan-out); compromise:
		\[
		\textbf{Xavier/Glorot (tanh/sig)}:\ \mathrm{Var}(w)=\frac{2}{n_{in}+n_{out}}, \qquad
		\textbf{He/Kaiming (ReLU)}:\ \mathrm{Var}(w)=\frac{2}{n_{in}}
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $c$ = activation ``gain'' (ReLU keeps $\approx$ half the signal $\Rightarrow$ factor 2). He has the extra $\sqrt2$ vs Xavier.
		\item $n_{in}+n_{out}$ = number of input and output connections
		\item PyTorch default: Kaiming (leaky ReLU), uniform; \texttt{torch.nn.init.\{xavier,kaiming\}\_\{normal,uniform\}\_}. Keras: \texttt{GlorotUniform}, \texttt{HeNormal}, \dots\ via \texttt{kernel\_initializer}.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Exploding Gradients: Gradient Clipping}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item When gradients explode (steep ``cliffs'' in the loss, common in \textbf{RNNs}), \textbf{clip} before the update: by \textbf{norm} (\texttt{clipnorm}) or per-component \textbf{value} (\texttt{clipvalue}).
		\item PyTorch: \texttt{torch.nn.utils.clip\_grad\_norm\_(model.parameters(), max\_norm)} between \texttt{backward()} and \texttt{step()}. Keras: optimiser args \texttt{clipnorm}/\texttt{clipvalue}.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Exam Tips}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Likely questions:
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
			\item \emph{Why do gradients vanish, and how to fix?} -- product of Jacobians shrinks with depth/saturation; fix with non-saturating activations, normalisation, good init (and ResNet skips).
			\item \emph{Why ReLU over sigmoid?} -- no saturation for $z>0$ $\rightarrow$ non-vanishing gradient; but watch \textbf{dying units} (use Leaky ReLU).
			\item \emph{Xavier vs He} -- Xavier $\frac{2}{n_{in}+n_{out}}$ for tanh/sigmoid; He $\frac{2}{n_{in}}$ for ReLU (extra factor 2). Be ready to state the formula.
			\item \emph{Write the BatchNorm equations}; explain train-vs-test difference (batch stats vs running averages) and the role of $\gamma,\beta$.
			\item \emph{Why normalise inputs?} -- dynamic range + data-independent init; standardisation vs min-max.
			\item \emph{Exploding gradients?} -- gradient clipping (esp.\ RNNs).
		\end{itemize}
		\textbf{Common mistakes:} forgetting BN behaves differently in \texttt{eval()} (running stats); swapping Xavier/He; thinking ReLU has \emph{no} failure mode (dying units); placing BN after the activation when the convention is before; normalising val/test with their \emph{own} stats instead of the training stats.
	\end{examtipbox}
	% ==================================================
	\section{Deep Learning Frameworks and Hardware Considerations}
	% ==================================================
	
	% --------------------------------------------------
	\subsection{Computational Graphs: the Foundation}
	% --------------------------------------------------

	\begin{definitionbox}
		A \textbf{computational graph} is a \textbf{directed acyclic graph (DAG)} where \emph{nodes} are operations/variables and \emph{edges} are data links. 3 variable types: \textbf{input} (incl.\ tunable parameters), \textbf{intermediary}, \textbf{output}. Node complexity is free: from a $+$ up to a full MLP layer.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\textbf{Why frameworks are built on graphs -- the 5 advantages:}
	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Intuitive backprop:} gradient of the output w.r.t.\ any parameter $=$ chain rule, by back-propagating the upstream gradient $\times$ the \emph{local} gradient of each node.
		\item \textbf{Easy to add new nodes:} just implement the \texttt{forward} / \texttt{backward} pair (local gradient) -- the rest is automatic.
		\item[3--4.] \textbf{Composition / factorisation:} any architecture is built from atomic nodes (no manual global gradient); a sub-graph can be \emph{fused} into one node if an analytic gradient exists (e.g.\ sigmoid).
		\item[5.] \textbf{Loss \& update rules are also nodes:} the loss $J$ plugs onto the end of the graph; gradients are back-propagated \emph{from} it.
	\end{enumerate}

	\textbf{Patterns in the backward flow} (interpreting each gate by what it does to the gradient):

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{8.2cm}@{}}
		\toprule
		Gate & Role & Backward behaviour \\
		\midrule
		$+$        & \textbf{distributor} & passes the same upstream gradient to all inputs \\
		$\times$   & \textbf{switcher}     & routes gradient $\times$ the \emph{other} input's value \\
		$(\cdot)^2$ & \textbf{amplifier} & amplifies/scales path \\
		$\max$-pool &  \textbf{router} & sends gradient only to the selected path \\
		fan-out (multi-output) & \textbf{cumulator} & incoming gradients \emph{add} at the node \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{importantbox}
		These patterns inspired architecture design: the \emph{distributor} ($+$) $\to$ skip connections in \textbf{ResNet}; the \emph{cumulator} (multi-output) $\to$ \textbf{GoogLeNet} multiple outputs; the \emph{router} ($\max$) $\to$ max-pooling routes all updates to the winning feature.
	\end{importantbox}

	\textbf{Graph folding / node fusion (optimisation):} a compiler (e.g.\ \textbf{XLA} for TF) rewrites a sub-graph into an equivalent, cheaper one -- e.g.\ $(x_1^2)(x_2^2) \to (x_1 x_2)^2$ saves one operator and one intermediary node.

	\begin{examtipbox}
		``\emph{Are computational graphs faster?}'' $\to$ \textbf{No, not by themselves.} They give an elegant OO forward/backward implementation; real speed-ups come from (a) graph \emph{compilation/optimisation} (fusion, fitting the processor) and (b) \textbf{tensors + batching} on fast CPU/GPU libraries. A backward node must \emph{store its forward values} $\Rightarrow$ training costs \textbf{more memory} than inference.
	\end{examtipbox}

	% --------------------------------------------------
	\subsection{Static vs.\ Dynamic (Eager) Execution}
	% --------------------------------------------------

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{@{}p{4cm} p{5.4cm} p{5.4cm}@{}}
		\toprule
		& \textbf{Static graph} (define-then-run) & \textbf{Dynamic / eager} (define-by-run) \\
		\midrule
		Execution & build $\to$ compile $\to$ run & ops run immediately \\
		Code      & less readable, harder to debug & readable, easy to debug, ``Pythonic'' \\
		Performance & usually \emph{better} (optimised) & usually \emph{lower} (built on the fly) \\
		Architecture & fixed once compiled & can change per iteration \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	% --------------------------------------------------
	\subsection{Deep Learning Frameworks}
	% --------------------------------------------------

	\textbf{The zoo} (\emph{italic = deprecated}): academia -- \emph{Theano}, \emph{Torch}, \emph{Caffe}, MXNet; companies -- TensorFlow (Google), PyTorch (Facebook/Meta), \emph{Caffe2}, \emph{CNTK} (Microsoft), PaddlePaddle (Baidu). Two big players remain: \textbf{TensorFlow} and \textbf{PyTorch}.

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{@{}p{2.7cm} p{5.3cm} p{5.3cm}@{}}
		\toprule
		& \textbf{TensorFlow} & \textbf{PyTorch} \\
		\midrule
		Origin    & Google, based on Theano & Facebook/Meta, based on Torch \\
		Initial mode & \textbf{static} graph (eager since 2.0) & \textbf{dynamic} ``eager'' (can compile) \\
		Both modes & yes & yes \\
		Learning curve & steeper $\to$ high-level API (Keras) & smoother, more ``Python native'' \\
		Best at   & \textbf{production}, scaling, edge/mobile & \textbf{prototyping}, research, training control \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{4 reasons to use a DL framework:} (1) build big computational graphs easily; (2) compute losses \& gradients for the update rule automatically; (3) ready-made SOTA regularisers/optimisers; (4) switch CPU$\leftrightarrow$GPU with one line.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\textbf{TensorFlow training pattern (idea, not syntax):}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \texttt{@tf.function} marks a function whose body \emph{defines} the graph (no compute until called).
		\item Graph \textbf{inputs} = function arguments (features $x$, weights $w$, targets $y$); \textbf{outputs} = predictions, loss, gradients fetched after running.
		\item Choose the device: \texttt{with tf.device('/GPU:0')}.
		\item \textbf{Pitfall:} updating weights with NumPy in the Python loop copies them \textbf{CPU$\leftrightarrow$GPU every step} $\Rightarrow$ slow. Fix: use a \texttt{tf.Variable} + a built-in optimiser so updates stay on the GPU.
	\end{itemize}

	% --------------------------------------------------
	\subsection{CPU vs.\ GPU}
	% --------------------------------------------------

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
		\toprule
		& \textbf{CPU} (Central) & \textbf{GPU} (Graphical) \\
		\midrule
		Cores       & few ($\sim$10) & many ($\sim$1000) \\
		Clock       & fast ($\sim$4\,GHz) & slower ($\sim$1.5\,GHz) \\
		Cache       & large & small \\
		Strength    & \textbf{sequential} tasks & \textbf{parallel} tasks \\
		Providers   & Intel, AMD & \textbf{NVIDIA}, AMD \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Why GPUs for DL:} the core op is \textbf{matrix multiplication} ($A\times B$, e.g.\ batch $\times$ inputs $\to$ logits). Every output cell is computed \emph{independently} $\Rightarrow$ ANNs are \textbf{``embarrassingly parallel''}, a perfect match for many slow cores.
	\end{importantbox}

	\textbf{Programming GPUs:}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{CUDA} -- NVIDIA only; low-level C-like API; optimised higher-level libs \texttt{cuBLAS}, \texttt{cuFFT}, \textbf{\texttt{cuDNN}}.
		\item \textbf{AMD ROCm} -- \texttt{HIP} ``write once'' + \texttt{hipify} converts CUDA $\to$ AMD.
		\item \textbf{OpenCL} -- runs on anything, but usually slower on NVIDIA.
		\item In practice: we call \emph{existing} GPU libraries from Python -- we don't write CUDA.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{GPU bottleneck:} disk$\to$GPU data transfer. Fixes: load data into RAM, fast SSDs, \textbf{CPU prefetch threads} (done by the framework), multi-GPU bus \textbf{NV-Link}.
		\item \textbf{Tensor cores} (NVIDIA Volta/V100, 2017+): hardware $4{\times}4{\times}4$ matrix multiply-accumulate (MMA) instead of scalar ops. Google's \textbf{TPU} is the same idea.
		\item \textbf{Backend network} for multi-server training: \emph{GPU fabric} (NV-Link) between boards, \emph{InfiniBand} between servers.
		\item \textbf{HW evolution:} V100 $\to$ A100 $\to$ H100/GH200 $\to$ \textbf{B200 (Blackwell)} -- more HBM memory \& bandwidth, native \textbf{FP4} ``Transformer Engine'', NVLink 5; clearly optimised for LLMs.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Likely Qs: ``\emph{Why are GPUs faster than CPUs for DL?}'' (parallel matrix mult, embarrassingly parallel -- \emph{not} faster per core); ``\emph{CPU vs GPU trade-offs?}'' (few fast cores vs many slow cores); ``\emph{What is the typical GPU bottleneck?}'' (data transfer, not compute). Don't say the graph itself is fast -- \textbf{tensors + batching} are.
	\end{examtipbox}

	% --------------------------------------------------
	\subsection{AI and Sustainability}
	% --------------------------------------------------

	\begin{definitionbox}
		Two distinct branches: \textbf{AI \emph{for} sustainability} = using AI to cut emissions (smart logistics, energy efficiency, predictive maintenance, peak-load adaption). \textbf{Sustainability \emph{of} AI} = the footprint of AI itself, split into \emph{environmental} (energy + carbon) and \emph{ethical} AI.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\textbf{Why it matters:} training compute grew $\sim\!4\times$/year in the deep-learning era; projections say IT may use up to \textbf{20\% of world electricity by 2030}, AI a big share. Inference cost $\propto$ energy. (EU \textbf{AI Act} + \textbf{CSRD} reporting push firms to disclose AI emissions.)

	\begin{formulabox}
		\[ \text{Emissions}_{\text{CO}_2} \;=\; \text{Energy}_{\text{consumed}} \times \text{Intensity}_{\text{carbon}} \]
		\[ \text{Energy}_{\text{process}} \;=\; \textstyle\sum \text{Runtime} \times \big(P_{\text{CPU}} + P_{\text{GPU}} + P_{\text{RAM}} + \dots\big) \]
		\[ \text{Energy}_{\text{consumed}} \;=\; \text{Energy}_{\text{process}} \times \text{PUE} \times \text{PSF} \]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Energy in \textbf{kWh}; carbon intensity in \textbf{gCO\textsubscript{2}eq/kWh}; emissions in \textbf{gCO\textsubscript{2}eq}.
		\item \textbf{Intensity} depends mostly on \emph{where} (country energy mix) the model runs.
		\item \textbf{PUE} (Power Usage Effectiveness) $= P_{\text{facility}}/P_{\text{IT}}$: overhead for cooling, lighting, delivery ($\geq 1$).
		\item \textbf{PSF} (Pragmatic Scaling Factor): how many times the computation is repeated (e.g.\ hyper-parameter tuning $=$ hundreds of trainings).
	\end{itemize}

	\textbf{4 levels to measure} (onion, inside $\to$ out): \textbf{L1} IT process / AI model $\to$ \textbf{L2} hardware (CPU/GPU/RAM) $\to$ \textbf{L3} data centre $\to$ \textbf{L4} geolocalised energy source. (L1$\leftrightarrow$L2 is tricky: GPU sharing, virtualisation overhead.)

	\textbf{How to measure a specific model -- 2 approaches:}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Metering tool} (plug power meter): measures the \emph{whole} server+GPU, but also idle time.
		\item \textbf{Software power models (SPM):} read HW counters -- \textbf{RAPL} (Intel CPU), \textbf{NVIDIA-SMI} (GPU), Apple \texttt{powermetrics}. Fine granularity, but \emph{underestimate} by $\sim$20--30\% (ignore cooling, AC/DC, board).
		\item Python libs: \textbf{CodeCarbon} (most complete; explicit object / context manager / \texttt{@track\_emissions} decorator; uses Electricity Maps / CO2 Signal), CarbonTracker, Eco2AI, experiment-impact-tracker; online estimators: Green Algorithms, ML CO\textsubscript{2} Impact.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{What DL engineers can do:} run jobs in \emph{low-carbon regions}; report metrics \& efficient configs; release efficient code/models; weigh energy cost vs.\ benefit. The \textbf{compression $\to$ compensation $\to$ efficiency} pattern (quantisation/pruning $\to$ (Q)LoRA fine-tune $\to$ efficient backend/routing) shrinks a 70B@300\,mWh model to 30B@100\,mWh at similar accuracy.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Examiner favourites: \emph{distinguish} ``AI for sustainability'' vs ``sustainability of AI'' (don't mix them up); ``\emph{how do you measure a model's energy/carbon?}'' -- give the \textbf{Emissions $=$ Energy $\times$ Intensity} formula and name \textbf{PUE} \& \textbf{PSF}; ``\emph{which factors matter?}'' -- the \textbf{4 levels} (esp.\ country energy mix). Note SPM tools \emph{underestimate} consumption.
	\end{examtipbox}

	% ==================================================
	\section{Recurrent Neural Networks}
	% ==================================================

	\subsection{Motivation: Which Architecture for Which Data?}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		Architectures encode a \textbf{prior} about how features relate. The right choice depends on whether features have a notion of \textbf{locality} or \textbf{sequential order}.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{center}
		\begin{tabular}{lll}
			\toprule
			Model & Best for & Notion of locality \\
			\midrule
			MLP & Arbitrary feature combinations & Ignores it -- flexible but inefficient \\
			CNN & Local features (incl.\ 1D, e.g.\ audio) & Exploits spatial/temporal locality \\
			RNN & Sequential data & Recurrence over time steps \\
			\bottomrule
		\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{CNN}: succeeds when features have \textbf{locality}; works on 1D data (audio) as well as 2D images.
		\item \textbf{MLP}: combines arbitrary features but is inefficient when features are related by locality (no weight sharing).
		\item \textbf{RNN}: designed for \textbf{sequential} data -- new elements are generated recurrently from a hidden state fed by previous outputs and new input.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Properties of Sequence Models}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Account for order}: elements are \emph{not} independent; sequential order matters. Must capture \textbf{local} and possibly \textbf{long-term} dependencies.
		\item \textbf{Parameter sharing}: the same parameters are reused across positions (like CNNs) -- efficient, and allows modelling sequences of \textbf{arbitrary length}.
		\item \textbf{Variable length}: handle inputs of differing length; in practice a fixed max length is set for training with \textbf{padding} (at least per batch).
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Core Concepts}
	
	\begin{definitionbox}
		An \textbf{RNN} processes sequences by maintaining a hidden state $h_t$ that summarizes past inputs. Weights are \textbf{shared across time steps}.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{General recurrent update:}
		\[
		h_t = f(h_{t-1}, x_t)
		\]
		The cell $A$ computes the new state from the previous state $h_{t-1}$ and the current input $x_t$ via a non-linear function $f$ (same $f$ at every step).
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $x_t$: input element at step $t$ (tensor-valued).
		\item $h_t$: hidden state at step $t$ (tensor-valued); also the output.
		\item $f$: non-linear update, parameters shared across all $t$.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzpicture}[>={Stealth[round]}, node distance=9mm]
			\node[circle, draw, fill=blue!25] (x) {$x_t$};
			\node[draw, fill=green!20, minimum width=11mm, minimum height=8mm, above=of x] (A) {$A$};
			\node[circle, draw, fill=violet!25, above=of A] (h) {$h_t$};
			\node[left=of A] (hp) {$h_{t-1}$};
			\draw[->] (x) -- (A);
			\draw[->] (hp) -- (A);
			\draw[->] (A) -- (h);
		\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}
	
	\begin{formulabox}
		\[
		h_t = \sigma\!\big(W_{hh} h_{t-1} + W_{xh} x_t + b\big), \qquad
		\hat y_t = W_{hy} h_t
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	
	\subsection{Elman Network (Vanilla RNN)}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		The \textbf{Elman network} is the simplest RNN: the hidden state is an \textbf{affine transform} of the current input and previous state, passed through a \textbf{non-linearity} (typically $\tanh$).
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\[
		h_t = f\big(W_{xh}\, x_t + W_{hh}\, h_{t-1} + b_h\big), \qquad f(z) = \tanh(z)
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $x_t$: input at step $t$, shape $n_x$; \quad $h_t$: hidden state, shape $n_h$.
		\item $W_{xh}$: input$\to$hidden weights, shape $n_h \times n_x$ (slide: $W_x$).
		\item $W_{hh}$: hidden$\to$hidden weights, shape $n_h \times n_h$ (slide: $W_h$).
		\item $b_h$: bias, shape $n_h$; \quad $f$: non-linear activation.
		\item Parameters $W_{xh}, W_{hh}, b_h$ are \textbf{shared across all steps}.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		Forward propagation = \textbf{affine transformation of the inputs} (as in an MLP) followed by a \textbf{non-linear activation}. The recurrence is the only new ingredient versus an MLP.
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Single-Layer RNN (Unrolled)}

	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzpicture}[
			>={Stealth[round]},
			cell/.style={draw, fill=green!20, minimum width=10mm, minimum height=8mm},
			inp/.style={circle, draw, fill=blue!25, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=0pt},
			hid/.style={circle, draw, fill=violet!25, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=0pt}]
			\node[cell] (A0) {$A$};
			\node[cell, right=14mm of A0] (A1) {$A$};
			\node[cell, right=14mm of A1] (A2) {$A$};
			\node[cell, right=22mm of A2] (At) {$A$};
			\foreach \i in {0,1,2,t} {
				\node[inp, below=9mm of A\i] (x\i) {$x_{\i}$};
				\node[hid, above=9mm of A\i] (h\i) {$h_{\i}$};
				\draw[->] (x\i) -- (A\i);
				\draw[->] (A\i) -- (h\i);
			}
			\node[left=12mm of A0] (init) {$h_{-1}$};
			\draw[->] (init) -- node[above, font=\scriptsize] {init} (A0);
			\draw[->] (A0) -- node[above, font=\scriptsize] {$h_0$} (A1);
			\draw[->] (A1) -- node[above, font=\scriptsize] {$h_1$} (A2);
			\draw[->] (A2) -- (At);
			\node at ($(A2)!0.5!(At)$) {$\cdots$};
			\node at ($(x2)!0.5!(xt)$) {$\cdots$};
		\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Updated through a \textbf{succession of steps}; each applies $h_t = f(h_{t-1}, x_t)$.
		\item \textbf{Same function $f$} at every step $\Rightarrow$ parameters shared across time.
		\item The state can \textbf{memorise} information from earlier in the sequence.
		\item State is passed to the next cell (step $t{+}1$) or up to \textbf{another layer}.
		\item State is \textbf{initialised} before the first step (often zeros), denoted $h_{-1}$.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Sequence Model Categories}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		Sequence models are categorised by the \textbf{length of input vs.\ output} sequence.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{center}
		\begin{tabular}{lll}
			\toprule
			Category & In $\to$ Out & Example \\
			\midrule
			one-to-many & $1 \to n$ & Image captioning (image $\to$ words) \\
			many-to-one & $n \to 1$ & Sentiment classification (words $\to$ label) \\
			many-to-many (aligned, 1:1) & $n \to n$ & Named-entity recognition (word $\to$ class) \\
			many-to-many (unaligned) & $n \to m$ & Translation, speech recognition, chatbots \\
			\bottomrule
		\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{importantbox}
		The two many-to-many types differ: \textbf{aligned} produces one output per input step (same length, 1:1) -- e.g.\ NER. \textbf{Unaligned} has input and output of \emph{different} lengths and needs an \textbf{encoder--decoder (seq2seq)} -- e.g.\ translation.
	\end{importantbox}

	
	\subsection{Implementation in PyTorch (\texttt{torch.nn.RNN})}

\begin{verbatim}
rnn = torch.nn.RNN(input_size, hidden_size, batch_first=True)
# x : (batch, steps, input_size)   input includes ALL steps
# h0: (batch, 1, hidden_size)      optional, default = zeros
output, hn = rnn(x, h0)
# output: (batch, steps, hidden_size)   hidden state of EVERY step
# hn    : (batch, 1, hidden_size)       hidden state of LAST step only
\end{verbatim}

	\begin{center}
		\begin{tabular}{lll}
			\toprule
			Tensor & Shape & Example \\
			\midrule
			\texttt{x} (input) & (batch, steps, input\_size) & (1, 5, 10) \\
			\texttt{h0} (init) & (batch, 1, hidden\_size) & (1, 1, 20) \\
			\texttt{output} (all) & (batch, steps, hidden\_size) & (1, 5, 20) \\
			\texttt{hn} (last) & (batch, 1, hidden\_size) & (1, 1, 20) \\
			\bottomrule
		\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
			\item \texttt{output} = hidden state of \textbf{all} cells; \texttt{hn} = hidden state of the \textbf{last} cell only.
			\item The input tensor carries an \textbf{extra axis for the steps}; \texttt{h0} mirrors it with a length-1 step axis.
			\item \texttt{batch\_first=True} puts the batch dimension first: \texttt{(batch, steps, $\cdot$)}.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{importantbox}

	\textbf{Manual loop (equivalent to the one-go call):}

\begin{verbatim}
ht = h0[:,0,:]                       # drop step axis -> (batch, hidden)
output1 = torch.zeros(steps, 1, hidden_size)
for t in range(steps):
    xt = x[:,t,:]                    # t-th input, shape (1, 10)
    out, ht = rnn(xt, ht)            # ht shape (1, 20): step axis collapsed
    output1[t] = out
# identical to rnn(x, h0) up to numerical precision (max diff ~1e-7)
\end{verbatim}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Calling \texttt{rnn(x, h0)} once (\emph{in one go}) is the same as looping over steps -- iterations happen internally.
		\item Per step, tensors \textbf{lose the step axis}: \texttt{xt} is \texttt{(1,10)}, \texttt{ht} is \texttt{(1,20)} -- both 2D.
	\end{itemize}

	\textbf{As a module (RNN + Linear head):}

\begin{verbatim}
class RecurrentNet(torch.nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, input_size, hidden_size, output_size):
        super().__init__()
        self.rnn    = torch.nn.RNN(input_size, hidden_size, batch_first=True)
        self.linear = torch.nn.Linear(hidden_size, output_size)
    def forward(self, x):
        _, h = self.rnn(x)
        return self.linear(h[-1])    # last hidden state -> output
\end{verbatim}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Two layers: \textbf{(Simple) RNN} $\to$ \textbf{Linear / Softmax} head.
		\item \texttt{h[-1]} = last hidden state $\Rightarrow$ \textbf{many-to-one} (one prediction per sequence).
		\item Can be packed into \texttt{torch.nn.Sequential} for convenience.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		The \textbf{Linear head is always present} -- it projects the hidden state to the task's output size:
		\[
		\text{out} = W\, h_{[-1]} + b, \qquad W \in \mathbb{R}^{n_{out} \times n_h}
		\]
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
			\item \texttt{hidden\_size} ($n_h$) is an \textbf{architectural} choice; \texttt{output\_size} ($n_{out}$) is \textbf{fixed by the task} (\#classes, or 1 for regression). The Linear bridges the two.
			\item \textbf{Softmax} is optional and goes \emph{after} Linear: it turns logits into probabilities without changing the dimension ($n_{out} \to n_{out}$).
			\item It is \textbf{Linear always, Softmax sometimes} -- not either/or.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Implementation in Keras (\texttt{SimpleRNN})}

\begin{verbatim}
model = Sequential()
model.add(SimpleRNN(units=n_hidden, input_shape=(maxlen, len_alphabet)))
model.add(Dense(n_languages, activation='softmax'))
model.compile(loss='categorical_crossentropy', optimizer='adam',
              metrics=['accuracy'])
model.summary()
\end{verbatim}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \texttt{units}: dimension of the hidden state ($n_h$).
		\item \texttt{input\_shape=(maxlen, len\_alphabet)}: sequence length $\times$ dimension of one element.
		\item \texttt{Dense(..., activation='softmax')}: classification head (e.g.\ into languages).
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{Parameter counts:}
		\[
		\text{SimpleRNN} = n_h\,(n_h + n_x + 1), \qquad
		\text{Dense} = n_{out}\,(n_h + 1)
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $n_h$: hidden units, \; $n_x$: input element dim, \; $n_{out}$: \#outputs; the $+1$ is the bias.
	\end{itemize}

	\textbf{Worked example} ($n_h=64$, $n_x=74$, $n_{out}=5$):
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tabular}{lll}
			\toprule
			Layer & Output shape & Params \\
			\midrule
			SimpleRNN(64) & (None, 64) & $64(64{+}74{+}1) = 8896$ \\
			Dense(5, softmax) & (None, 5) & $5(64{+}1) = 325$ \\
			\midrule
			\textbf{Total} & & \textbf{9221} \\
			\bottomrule
		\end{tabular}
	\end{center}
	Breakdown of the 8896: $W_{xh}\,(64{\times}74){=}4736$, $W_{hh}\,(64{\times}64){=}4096$, $b\,(64){=}64$.

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Significant parameter sharing}: the RNN reuses the \emph{same} weights at every time step, so the count is \textbf{independent of sequence length}. An equivalent MLP (with \texttt{maxlen=18}) would need $\approx 85{,}312$ params -- vs.\ $9221$ here.
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Stacking RNNs (Deep RNNs)}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{Stacked / deep RNN}: several RNN layers on top of each other. The sequence of hidden states from layer $l$ is the \textbf{input} to layer $l{+}1$ at every time step -- depth builds more abstract temporal features.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzpicture}[
			>={Stealth[round]},
			cell/.style={draw, fill=green!20, minimum width=9mm, minimum height=7mm},
			inp/.style={circle, draw, fill=blue!25, minimum size=6mm, inner sep=0pt}]
			% layer 1
			\node[cell] (a0) {$A$};
			\node[cell, right=12mm of a0] (a1) {$A$};
			\node[cell, right=12mm of a1] (a2) {$A$};
			\node[cell, right=18mm of a2] (at) {$A$};
			% layer 2 (above) + inputs
			\foreach \i in {0,1,2,t} {
				\node[cell, above=12mm of a\i] (b\i) {$A$};
				\node[inp, below=7mm of a\i] (x\i) {$x_{\i}$};
				\draw[->] (x\i) -- (a\i);
				\draw[->] (a\i) -- (b\i);
			}
			% horizontal recurrence
			\draw[->] (a0) -- (a1); \draw[->] (a1) -- (a2); \draw[->] (a2) -- (at);
			\draw[->] (b0) -- (b1); \draw[->] (b1) -- (b2); \draw[->] (b2) -- (bt);
			% head
			\node[draw, fill=green!30, above=7mm of bt, align=center] (sm) {softmax\\/ linear};
			\draw[->] (bt) -- (sm);
			% ellipses
			\node at ($(a2)!0.5!(at)$) {$\cdots$};
			\node at ($(b2)!0.5!(bt)$) {$\cdots$};
			\node at ($(x2)!0.5!(xt)$) {$\cdots$};
			% layer labels
			\node[left=3mm of a0] {\footnotesize Layer 1};
			\node[left=3mm of b0] {\footnotesize Layer 2};
		\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}

\begin{verbatim}
model.add(SimpleRNN(n_hidden, input_shape=(maxlen, len_alphabet),
                    return_sequences=True))    # layer 1
model.add(SimpleRNN(n_hidden, return_sequences=True))    # layer 2
model.add(SimpleRNN(n_hidden, return_sequences=False))   # last layer
model.add(Dense(n_languages, activation='softmax'))
\end{verbatim}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
			\item \texttt{return\_sequences=True} (lower layers): output the state at \textbf{every} time step, so the full sequence is passed to the next layer.
			\item \texttt{return\_sequences=False} (last RNN layer): output \textbf{only the last} state $\Rightarrow$ feeds the \texttt{Dense}/softmax head (many-to-one).
			\item \textbf{PyTorch}: use the \texttt{num\_layers} argument of \texttt{nn.RNN} to stack directly.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Training: BPTT}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Backprop Through Time}: unroll the network and backprop over steps.
		\item Suffers from vanishing/exploding gradients over long sequences.
		\item \textbf{Gradient clipping} controls exploding gradients.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Backprop with a Single-Layer RNN}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		BPTT = \textbf{unroll} the RNN, then apply the \textbf{chain rule} backward. The loss (cross-entropy on the softmax output) flows back through the output head, then \textbf{backward through time} via the hidden states.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{center}
		\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{images_summary/bptt_single_layer_rnn.png}
	\end{center}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{Recurrence Jacobian} (the key term), with $z_s = W_x x_s + W_h h_{s-1} + b_h$:
		\[
		\frac{\partial h_s}{\partial h_{s-1}} = W_h^\top \, g'(z_s)
		\]
		\textbf{Shared params} $\Rightarrow$ sum over all steps (full BPTT gradient):
		\[
		\frac{\partial L_{ce}}{\partial W_h}
		= \frac{\partial L_{ce}}{\partial \hat y}\,\frac{\partial \hat y}{\partial h_t}
		\sum_{s=1}^{t+1}\underbrace{\left(\prod_{\tau=s}^{t}\frac{\partial h_\tau}{\partial h_{\tau-1}}\right)}_{\text{chain back through time}}\frac{\partial h_{s-1}}{\partial W_h}
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $g = f$: the activation; $g'(z_s)$: its derivative (diagonal). \; $W_h^\top$: transposed recurrent weights.
		\item All partials ($\partial h_t/\partial W_x,\,\partial h_t/\partial W_h,\,\partial h_t/\partial b_h$) are vectors/matrices -- straightforward per cell.
		\item Output head: $\hat y = \mathrm{softmax}(W_s h_t + b_s)$; backprop starts at $\partial L_{ce}/\partial \hat y$.
		\item Gradient from step $t$ back to step $k$ is a \textbf{product} of Jacobians $\prod_{s} W_h^\top g'(z_s)$.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		That long \textbf{product of $W_h^\top g'(z_s)$ terms} is exactly why gradients \textbf{vanish} (factors $<1$) or \textbf{explode} (factors $>1$) over long sequences -- motivating gradient clipping and gated units (LSTM/GRU).
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Gradient Clipping}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		For \textbf{exploding gradients}: \textbf{clip the gradient before the update step} so a single step cannot become huge.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{clipnorm}: clip the \emph{length (norm)} of the whole gradient vector.
		\item \textbf{clipvalue}: clip the \emph{absolute value} of each component individually.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Cliff intuition} (Goodfellow): composite multiplication of weights creates extremely steep regions (\emph{cliffs}). At a cliff the gradient becomes very large, so even a \emph{small} step jumps far off course. Clipping bounds the step and keeps training stable.
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Long-Term Dependencies: Captured but Hard to Learn}

	\begin{importantbox}
		Simple RNNs are \textbf{Turing-complete} -- in principle they can approximate any algorithm and keep arbitrary (incl.\ long-term) memory. \textbf{In practice} they fail to \emph{learn} long-term dependencies due to \textbf{vanishing/exploding gradients}.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Capacity vs.\ learnability}: the representational power exists; gradient-based training is what breaks down.
		\item Problem identified by \textbf{Hochreiter \& Schmidhuber (1991)} and \textbf{Bengio, Simard \& Frasconi (1994)}.
	\end{itemize}

	\textbf{Attempts to solve it:}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{LSTM} cells (Hochreiter \& Schmidhuber, 1997).
		\item \textbf{GRU} (Cho et al., 2014).
		\item Suitable \textbf{weight initialisation}.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Long-Term Memory and Gating (Intro to LSTM/GRU)}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		Two ideas fix the vanishing-gradient problem of vanilla RNNs:
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
			\item \textbf{Long memory (pass-through)}: a direct path for the recurrent state (like a \textbf{ResNet residual connection}) so it is \emph{not} repeatedly squashed through a non-linearity.
			\item \textbf{Gating}: update the state as a \textbf{weighted average} of its previous value and a candidate update; the weight (a ``forget gate'') depends on input and state $\Rightarrow$ \textbf{multiplicative} structure.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{formulabox}
		Simplest gating cell:
		\[
		z_t = \sigma(W_z x_t + U_z h_{t-1}),\qquad
		\tilde h_t = f(W x_t + U h_{t-1})
		\]
		\[
		h_t = z_t \odot h_{t-1} + (1-z_t)\odot \tilde h_t
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $z_t$: update/forget gate, $\sigma$ (sigmoid) $\Rightarrow z_t\in(0,1)$. \quad $\odot$: element-wise multiplication.
		\item $\tilde h_t$: candidate state from a \textbf{simple cell} ($f=\tanh$ or relu). \quad $h_{t-1}$: previous state (pass-through).
		\item $z_t\!\to\!1$: keep old state (remember); $z_t\!\to\!0$: overwrite with candidate (update). This is the core of \textbf{GRU}; LSTM splits it into separate input/forget/output gates with a cell state $c_t$.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[
		>={Stealth[round]},
		box/.style={draw, rounded corners, fill=blue!12, minimum height=7mm, minimum width=16mm, align=center, font=\footnotesize},
		dot/.style={circle, fill=black, inner sep=1.3pt},
		every node/.style={font=\footnotesize},
		]
		% nodes
		\node (in)   at (0,0)        {$h_{t-1}$};
		\node[dot] (split) at (1,0)  {};
		\node[box] (gate) at (5.3,0.4)  {weighted avg\\(gate)};
		\node[dot] (join) at (6.6,0.4)  {};
		\node (out)  at (8.6,0.4)    {$h_t$};
		\node[box] (sig)  at (2.4,-0.4) {$\sigma$: compute\\weight};
		\node[box] (cell) at (3.6,-1.6) {simple cell\\(relu, tanh)};
		\node (x)    at (3.0,-3.0)   {$x_t$};
		\node[dot] (xb) at (3.0,-2.5) {};
		% pass-through (top) into gate
		\draw[->, thick] (in) -- (split);
		\draw[->, thick] (split) |- (gate.west);
		% h_{t-1} into sub-cells
		\draw[->] (split) |- (sig.west);
		\draw[->] (split) |- (cell.west);
		% x_t into sub-cells
		\draw[->, red] (x) -- (xb);
		\draw[->, red] (xb) -| (sig.south);
		\draw[->, red] (xb) -| (cell.south);
		% sub-cell outputs into gate
		\draw[->] (sig.east)  -| node[pos=0.25,above]{$z_t$} ($(gate.south)+(-0.3,0)$);
		\draw[->] (cell.east) -| node[pos=0.25,above]{$\tilde h_t$} ($(gate.south)+(0.3,0)$);
		% gate -> out
		\draw[->, thick] (gate.east) -- (join) -- (out.west);
		% cell boundary
		\begin{scope}[on background layer]
		\node[draw=green!50!black, fill=green!8, rounded corners,
		      fit={(sig) (cell) (gate) (split) (xb)}, inner sep=5pt] {};
		\end{scope}
	\end{tikzpicture}\\[2pt]
	{\footnotesize Simplest form of a gating cell: \quad $h_t = z_t\odot h_{t-1} + (1-z_t)\odot \tilde h_t$ \;(element-wise).}
	\end{center}

	\subsection{Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM)}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{LSTM} (Hochreiter \& Schmidhuber, \textbf{1997}): same cyclic RNN structure, but the cell carries \textbf{two} states -- it adds a \textbf{long-term memory} $c_t$ (cell state) alongside the hidden state $h_t$.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
		\toprule
		\textbf{State} & \textbf{Role} & \textbf{Flows} \\
		\midrule
		$c_t$ & long-term memory (cell state) & $c_{t-1}\to c_t$ \\
		$h_t$ & short-term / working memory + output & $h_{t-1}\to h_t$ \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[
		>={Stealth[round]},
		every node/.style={font=\footnotesize},
		]
		\node[draw=green!50!black, fill=green!10, rounded corners, minimum width=28mm, minimum height=20mm] (cell) {LSTM};
		% long-term memory line (top)
		\node[left=10mm of cell.west, yshift=4mm]  (cin)  {$c_{t-1}$};
		\node[right=10mm of cell.east, yshift=4mm] (cout) {$c_t$};
		\node[right=1mm of cout] {long-term memory};
		\draw[->] (cin) -- (cin-|cell.west);
		\draw[->] (cell.east|-cout) -- (cout);
		% short-term / working memory line (bottom)
		\node[left=10mm of cell.west, yshift=-4mm]  (hin)  {$h_{t-1}$};
		\node[right=10mm of cell.east, yshift=-4mm] (hout) {$h_t$};
		\node[right=1mm of hout] {short-term / working memory};
		\draw[->] (hin) -- (hin-|cell.west);
		\draw[->] (cell.east|-hout) -- (hout);
		% input
		\node[below=8mm of cell.south, fill=blue!20, circle, inner sep=1.5pt] (x) {$x_t$};
		\draw[->] (x) -- (cell.south);
		% output h_t (top)
		\node[above=8mm of cell.north, fill=violet!25, circle, inner sep=1.5pt] (hy) {$h_t$};
		\draw[->] (cell.north) -- (hy);
	\end{tikzpicture}\\[2pt]
	{\footnotesize LSTM unit: two memory channels in/out, plus input $x_t$; $h_t$ is also the output.}
	\end{center}

	\subsection{Internals of an LSTM Cell}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		The long-term memory $c_t$ is updated through \textbf{gates} (the $\times$ nodes). A gate controls how information flows between the short-term state $h_{t-1}$, the input $x_t$, and the long-term state $c_t$. Three gates:
		\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, nosep]
			\item \textbf{Forget gate} $f_t$ -- how much of $c_{t-1}$ to keep.
			\item \textbf{Input gate} $i_t$ -- how much of the candidate $\tilde c_t$ to write.
			\item \textbf{Output gate} $o_t$ -- how much of $\tanh(c_t)$ to expose as $h_t$.
		\end{enumerate}
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{formulabox}
		Each gate = \textbf{affine transform} of $[x_t, h_{t-1}]$ + \textbf{activation}:
		\[
		f_t = \sigma(W_f x_t + U_f h_{t-1} + b_f),\quad
		i_t = \sigma(W_i x_t + U_i h_{t-1} + b_i),\quad
		o_t = \sigma(W_o x_t + U_o h_{t-1} + b_o)
		\]
		\[
		\tilde c_t = \tanh(W_c x_t + U_c h_{t-1} + b_c)
		\]
		\textit{Equivalent split-subscript notation} ($W_{\bullet,x}\equiv W_\bullet,\; W_{\bullet,h}\equiv U_\bullet$), e.g.:
		\[
		f_t = \sigma\!\left(W_{f,x}\cdot x_t + W_{f,h}\cdot h_{t-1} + b_f\right),\qquad
		o_t = \sigma\!\left(W_{o,x}\cdot x_t + W_{o,h}\cdot h_{t-1} + b_o\right)
		\]
		\textbf{State updates} (element-wise $\odot$):
		\[
		c_t = f_t \odot c_{t-1} + i_t \odot \tilde c_t,
		\qquad
		h_t = o_t \odot \tanh(c_t)
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $f_t, i_t, o_t \in (0,1)^d$: gate vectors ($\sigma$). \quad $\tilde c_t$: candidate cell content ($\tanh$).
		\item $W_\bullet, U_\bullet, b_\bullet$: per-gate weights/bias (4 sets, all learned). \quad $\odot$: element-wise product.
		\item Gates implemented by: \textbf{affine transform} $\to$ \textbf{activation} $\to$ \textbf{element-wise multiplication}.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[
		>={Stealth[round]}, font=\footnotesize,
		op/.style={circle, draw, fill=red!18, inner sep=0pt, minimum size=5mm},
		gate/.style={draw, fill=yellow!45, minimum width=7mm, minimum height=5mm, font=\scriptsize},
		ell/.style={draw, ellipse, fill=red!12, inner sep=1pt, minimum width=9mm, font=\scriptsize},
		]
		% cell-state (top) line
		\node (cin)  at (-0.6,1.2) {$c_{t-1}$};
		\node (cout) at ( 7.4,1.2) {$c_t$};
		\node[op] (m1)   at (1.5,1.2) {$\times$};
		\node[op] (plus) at (3.5,1.2) {$+$};
		% intermediate ops
		\node[op]  (m2) at (3.5,0.3) {$\times$};
		\node[ell] (to) at (5.6,0.6) {tanh};
		\node[op]  (m3) at (5.6,-0.1){$\times$};
		% gates
		\node[gate] (gf) at (1.5,-0.7) {$\sigma$};
		\node[gate] (gi) at (3.0,-0.7) {$\sigma$};
		\node[gate] (gc) at (4.0,-0.7) {tanh};
		\node[gate] (go) at (5.2,-0.7) {$\sigma$};
		% gate index tags
		\node[red,font=\scriptsize] at ($(m1)+(0.45,0.32)$) {1};
		\node[red,font=\scriptsize] at ($(m2)+(0.5,0.3)$) {2};
		\node[red,font=\scriptsize] at ($(m3)+(0.55,0.18)$) {3};
		% hidden-state (bottom) input line + output
		\node (hin)  at (-0.6,-1.5) {$h_{t-1}$};
		\node (hout) at ( 7.4,-1.5) {$h_t$};
		\node[circle, fill=blue!25, inner sep=1.5pt] (x)  at (1.0,-2.6) {$x_t$};
		\node[circle, fill=violet!30, inner sep=1.5pt] (hy) at (6.4,2.2) {$h_t$};
		% --- top line ---
		\draw[->,thick] (cin) -- (m1);
		\draw[->,thick] (m1) -- (plus);
		\draw[->,thick] (plus) -- (cout);
		% forget gate
		\draw[->] (gf) -- (m1) node[pos=0.45, right=1pt]{$f_t$};
		% input path
		\draw[->] (gi) |- node[pos=0.2, left=1pt]{$i_t$} (m2.west);
		\draw[->] (gc) |- node[pos=0.2, right=1pt]{$\tilde c_t$} (m2.east);
		\draw[->] (m2) -- (plus);
		% output path
		\draw[->] (5.6,1.2) -- (to);
		\draw[->] (to) -- (m3);
		\draw[->] (go) |- node[pos=0.2, left=1pt]{$o_t$} (m3.west);
		\draw[->,thick] (m3) -| (hy);
		\draw[->,thick] (m3) |- (hout);
		% input bus (h_{t-1} + x_t feed all gates)
		\draw[thick] (hin) -- (5.2,-1.5);
		\draw[thick] (x) -- (1.0,-1.5);
		\draw[->] (1.5,-1.5) -- (gf.south);
		\draw[->] (3.0,-1.5) -- (gi.south);
		\draw[->] (4.0,-1.5) -- (gc.south);
		\draw[->] (5.2,-1.5) -- (go.south);
		% boundary
		\begin{scope}[on background layer]
		\node[draw=green!50!black, fill=green!8, rounded corners,
		      fit={(m1) (plus) (gf) (go) (to) (m3) (hin)}, inner sep=6pt] {};
		\end{scope}
	\end{tikzpicture}\\[2pt]
	{\footnotesize Internals of an LSTM cell: gates 1 = forget, 2 = input, 3 = output.}
	\end{center}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Multiplicative gating} ($i_t\odot\tilde c_t$, etc.) multiplies two factors that both depend on the input -- an interaction \textbf{not} present in MLPs, CNNs, or simple RNNs. This is what lets the cell selectively keep/erase memory.
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Backprop with LSTMs: the Gradient Super-Highway}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		The cell-state line $c$ is a \textbf{``super-highway for backprop''}: the gradient flows backward along $c$ across many steps almost unobstructed -- at each step it is only \textbf{multiplied by the forget gate} $f_t$, with \emph{no} weight-matrix product and \emph{no} saturating non-linearity on this path.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{formulabox}
		From the additive cell update:
		\[
		c_t = f_t \odot c_{t-1} + i_t \odot \tilde c_t
		\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
		\frac{\partial c_t}{\partial c_{t-1}} = f_t
		\]
		Gradient from step $t$ back to step $s$:
		\[
		\frac{\partial c_t}{\partial c_s}
		= \prod_{\tau=s+1}^{t} \frac{\partial c_\tau}{\partial c_{\tau-1}}
		= \prod_{\tau=s+1}^{t} f_\tau
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $f_\tau \in (0,1)$: forget gate, \textbf{time-dependent} and \textbf{learned} per step.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[
		>={Stealth[round]}, font=\footnotesize,
		cell/.style={draw=green!55!black, fill=green!12, rounded corners,
		             minimum width=2cm, minimum height=1.3cm},
		]
		\node[cell] (A) at (0,0)   {};
		\node[cell] (B) at (3.4,0) {};
		\node[cell] (C) at (6.8,0) {};
		\foreach \n in {A,B,C}{ \node at (\n.center) {\scriptsize LSTM}; }
		% cell-state highway (top) + forget multiply markers
		\draw[very thick] (-1.7,0.5) -- (8.5,0.5);
		\foreach \x in {0,3.4,6.8}{ \node[font=\tiny] at (\x-0.45,0.72) {$\times f$}; }
		\node[left]  at (-1.7,0.5) {$c_{t-2}$};
		\node[font=\footnotesize] at (1.7,0.72)  {$c_{t-1}$};
		\node[font=\footnotesize] at (5.1,0.72)  {$c_t$};
		\node[right] at (8.5,0.5)  {$c_{t+1}$};
		% backward gradient arrows (the "highway")
		\foreach \a/\b in {1.1/-1.7, 4.5/2.3, 7.9/5.7}{
			\draw[->, red, very thick] (\a,0.95) -- (\b,0.95); }
		% hidden-state line (bottom) + outputs up
		\draw[thick] (-1.7,-0.5) -- (8.5,-0.5);
		\node[left]  at (-1.7,-0.5) {$h_{t-2}$};
		\node[right] at (8.5,-0.5) {$h_{t+1}$};
		\foreach \x/\lab in {0/{h_{t-1}}, 3.4/{h_t}, 6.8/{h_{t+1}}}{
			\draw[->] (\x,0.65) -- (\x,1.4); \node[above] at (\x,1.4) {$\lab$}; }
		\node[red, font=\bfseries] at (3.4,2.15) {``Super-Highway for Backprop''};
	\end{tikzpicture}\\[2pt]
	{\footnotesize Unrolled LSTMs: the cell state $c$ is a near-uninterrupted path for the backward gradient.}
	\end{center}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Why this fixes vanishing/exploding gradients:}
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
			\item \textbf{Vanilla RNN}: gradient $=\prod_\tau W_h^\top g'(z_\tau)$ -- the \emph{same} weight matrix repeated $\Rightarrow$ eigenvalues to the power of the sequence length $\Rightarrow$ exponential \textbf{vanish} ($<1$) or \textbf{explode} ($>1$), and the user cannot control it.
			\item \textbf{LSTM cell path}: gradient $=\prod_\tau f_\tau$ -- a product of \textbf{forget gates the network learns}. Set $f_\tau\!\approx\!1$ and $\partial c_t/\partial c_s\!\approx\!1$: the gradient passes back \textbf{unchanged} over long ranges (a \emph{constant error carousel}, like a ResNet skip connection).
			\item No weight-matrix multiply and no saturating non-linearity on this path $\Rightarrow$ vanishing is largely avoided; $f_\tau\in(0,1)$ keeps the product $\le 1$ $\Rightarrow$ no explosion either.
			\item \textbf{Gotcha}: $f_\tau\!\to\!0$ \emph{does} still vanish -- but only where the model \textbf{chose to forget} (no dependency to carry there), \textbf{not} forced everywhere as in a vanilla RNN. Initialising the forget-gate bias $b_f$ large positive makes $f_\tau\!\approx\!1$ \textbf{by default}, so gradients flow unless the cell learns to close the gate.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU)}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{GRU} (Cho et al., 2014): the most popular LSTM variant. Differences from LSTM:
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, nosep]
			\item \textbf{No separate long-term memory} $c_t$ -- everything lives in $h_t$.
			\item \textbf{Forget + input gates merged} into one \textbf{update gate} $u_t$.
			\item \textbf{Reset (relevance) gate} $r_t$ -- how much past state enters the candidate.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\[
		r_t = \sigma(W_{rh}\,h_{t-1} + W_{rx}\,x_t + b_r),\qquad
		u_t = \sigma(W_{uh}\,h_{t-1} + W_{ux}\,x_t + b_u)
		\]
		\[
		\tilde h_t = \tanh\!\big(W_{ch}\,(r_t \odot h_{t-1}) + W_{cx}\,x_t + b_c\big)
		\]
		\[
		h_t = (1-u_t)\odot h_{t-1} + u_t \odot \tilde h_t
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $r_t$: reset/relevance gate -- gates $h_{t-1}$ \emph{inside} the candidate. \quad $u_t$: update gate -- interpolates old state vs.\ candidate.
		\item $\tilde h_t$: candidate state. \quad $u_t\!\to\!0$: keep $h_{t-1}$; $u_t\!\to\!1$: take candidate.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[
		>={Stealth[round]}, font=\footnotesize,
		op/.style={circle, draw, fill=red!18, inner sep=0pt, minimum size=5mm},
		gate/.style={draw, fill=yellow!45, minimum width=7mm, minimum height=5mm, font=\scriptsize},
		]
		% hidden-state (top) line
		\node (hin)  at (-0.9,1.3) {$h_{t-1}$};
		\node (hout) at ( 6.0,1.3) {$h_t$};
		\node[op] (mtop) at (3.0,1.3) {$\times$};
		\node[op] (plus) at (4.4,1.3) {$+$};
		% intermediate ops
		\node[op] (om) at (3.0,0.5) {\scriptsize $1{-}$};
		\node[op] (mu) at (4.4,0.4) {$\times$};
		\node[op] (mr) at (0.6,-0.35) {$\times$};
		% gates
		\node[gate] (gr) at (0.6,-0.9) {$\sigma$};
		\node[gate] (gu) at (2.4,-0.9) {$\sigma$};
		\node[gate] (gc) at (3.6,-0.9) {tanh};
		% input
		\node[circle, fill=blue!25, inner sep=1.5pt] (x)  at (1.5,-2.7) {$x_t$};
		\node[circle, fill=violet!30, inner sep=1.5pt] (hy) at (4.4,2.3) {$h_t$};
		% top line
		\draw[->,thick] (hin) -- (mtop);
		\draw[->,thick] (mtop) -- (plus);
		\draw[->,thick] (plus) -- (hout);
		\draw[->,thick] (plus) -- (hy);
		% h_{t-1} branch down to reset mult
		\node[circle,fill=black,inner sep=1pt] (sp) at (-0.3,1.3) {};
		\draw[thick] (sp) -- (-0.3,-1.7);
		\draw[->] (-0.3,-0.35) -- (mr.west);
		% reset path: r_t * h_{t-1} -> candidate
		\draw[->] (gr) -- (mr) node[pos=0.78, right=2pt]{$r_t$};
		\draw[->] (mr) -| (gc.north);
		% candidate -> mu
		\draw[->] (gc.east) -| node[pos=0.25, above]{$\tilde h_t$} (mu.south);
		% update gate -> (1-) and mu
		\node[circle,fill=black,inner sep=1pt] (du) at (2.4,0.1) {};
		\draw[->] (gu) -- (du) node[pos=0.55, left=2pt]{\textcolor{red}{$u_t$}};
		\draw[->] (du) |- (om.west);
		\draw[->] (du) -| (mu.west);
		% (1-) -> mtop ; mu -> plus
		\draw[->] (om) -- (mtop);
		\draw[->] (mu) -- (plus);
		% input bus (h_{t-1}+x_t feed gates)
		\draw[thick] (x) -- (1.5,-1.7);
		\draw[thick] (-0.3,-1.7) -- (3.6,-1.7);
		\draw[->] (0.6,-1.7) -- (gr.south);
		\draw[->] (2.4,-1.7) -- (gu.south);
		\draw[->] (3.6,-1.7) -- (gc.south);
		% boundary
		\begin{scope}[on background layer]
		\node[draw=green!50!black, fill=green!8, rounded corners,
		      fit={(mtop) (plus) (gr) (gc) (mr) (mu) (om)}, inner sep=6pt] {};
		\end{scope}
	\end{tikzpicture}\\[2pt]
	{\footnotesize GRU cell: reset gate $r_t$ inside the candidate; update gate $u_t$ blends $h_{t-1}$ and $\tilde h_t$.}
	\end{center}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Fewer parameters than LSTM}: only \textbf{3} weight matrices of size $n_h\times(n_h+n_x)$ ($W_r, W_c, W_u$) vs.\ LSTM's 4:
		\[
		\text{GRU} = 3\,n_h\,(n_h + n_x + 1)
		\]
		Often comparable accuracy to LSTM at lower cost.
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Implementation in Keras (\texttt{LSTM})}

	\textbf{Human Activity Recognition} example: single LSTM layer, classify on the \textbf{last} cell's output ($\Rightarrow$ \texttt{return\_sequences=False}).

\begin{verbatim}
n_steps  = len(X_train[0])      # 128 timesteps per series
n_input  = len(X_train[0][0])   # 9 input features per timestep
n_hidden = 32                   # hidden state dimension
n_classes = 6

model = Sequential()
model.add(LSTM(units=n_hidden, return_sequences=False,
               input_shape=(n_steps, n_input)))
model.add(Dense(n_classes, activation='softmax'))
model.compile(loss='categorical_crossentropy', optimizer='adam',
              metrics=['accuracy'])
\end{verbatim}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Defaults: \texttt{activation=tanh} (candidate), \texttt{recurrent\_activation=sigmoid} (gates).
		\item \texttt{return\_sequences}: \texttt{False} $\to$ last output only (many-to-one); \texttt{True} $\to$ output at every step (many-to-many / stacking).
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{LSTM parameter count} -- \textbf{4 gates} ($k=f,i,c,o$), each a $W_k$ and bias $b_k$:
		\[
		\text{LSTM} = 4\,\big[\,n_h(n_h + n_x) + n_h\,\big] = 4\,n_h\,(n_h + n_x + 1)
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $n_h$: hidden units, \; $n_x$: input feature dim; the $+1$ is the per-gate bias.
		\item Each gate stacks $W_{k}=[n_h\times(n_h+n_x)]$ (acts on $[h_{t-1},x_t]$) and $b_k=[n_h\times1]$.
	\end{itemize}

	\textbf{Worked example} ($n_h=32$, $n_x=9$, $n_{out}=6$):
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tabular}{lll}
			\toprule
			Layer & Output shape & Params \\
			\midrule
			LSTM(32) & (None, 32) & $4\cdot 32(32{+}9{+}1) = 5376$ \\
			Dense(6, softmax) & (None, 6) & $6(32{+}1) = 198$ \\
			\midrule
			\textbf{Total} & & \textbf{5574} \\
			\bottomrule
		\end{tabular}
	\end{center}
	Per gate: $W_k\,[32{\times}(32{+}9)]{=}1312$, $b_k\,[32]{=}32 \Rightarrow 1344$; $\times 4 = 5376$.

	\begin{importantbox}
		An LSTM has \textbf{4$\times$} the parameters of a vanilla RNN of the same size (4 gates vs.\ 1). Count is still \textbf{independent of sequence length} (weight sharing across time).
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Regularisation in RNNs}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Batch-norm \emph{across time} is not reasonable}:
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, nosep]
			\item The activation distribution \textbf{should} be allowed to change over time steps.
			\item The \textbf{same} scale parameter reused at every step can cause \textbf{exploding gradients}.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Batch-norm in the vertical direction is fine}: apply it \textbf{between stacked RNN layers} (depth), not along the time axis.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{Dropout in RNNs} -- two variants, both using the \textbf{same mask at every time step}:
	\end{definitionbox}
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
			\toprule
			\textbf{Keras keyword} & \textbf{Mask applied to} & \textbf{Note} \\
			\midrule
			\texttt{dropout} (or \texttt{Dropout} layer) & input / output units & feed-forward connections \\
			\texttt{recurrent\_dropout} & hidden-state units & recurrent connections \\
			\bottomrule
		\end{tabular}
	\end{center}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Same mask across all time steps} (variational dropout) -- a fresh per-step mask would wipe out the memory signal.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Handling Class Imbalance}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{Class imbalance}: some classes have far more samples than others. The model can score high \textbf{accuracy} by predicting the majority and ignoring rare classes -- so accuracy is misleading; minority \textbf{recall} suffers.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{center}
		\begin{tabular}{@{}p{3.0cm}p{4.3cm}p{4.6cm}@{}}
			\toprule
			\textbf{Treatment} & \textbf{Mechanism} & \textbf{Trade-off} \\
			\midrule
			Minority \textbf{over-sampling} & Duplicate minority rows up to the majority count (sample \emph{with replacement}) & Balances data, model-agnostic; bigger set, \textbf{duplicates risk overfitting} \\
			\textbf{Class weights} in loss & Weight per-class loss $\propto 1/\text{freq}$ (Keras \texttt{class\_weight}) & No extra data, fast; adds no new info, noisier gradients \\
			(Under-sampling) & Drop majority rows & Smaller/faster; \textbf{throws away data} \\
			\bottomrule
		\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\textbf{Over-sampling, in code} (resample each minority class to \texttt{group\_size} $=$ majority count):
\begin{verbatim}
group_size = names['lang'].value_counts().max()   # size of majority class
lang_upsampled = resample(lang_names, replace=True,    # WITH replacement
                          n_samples=group_size,        # match majority
                          random_state=123)            # reproducible
\end{verbatim}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Pitfall -- data leakage}: over-sample \textbf{only the training set, after} the train/test split. Otherwise duplicated rows appear in both train \emph{and} test, inflating the metrics. (\texttt{class\_weight} avoids this -- the data is untouched.)
	\end{importantbox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \texttt{replace=True} is required: a minority class has fewer unique rows than \texttt{group\_size}.
		\item Report \textbf{per-class recall / macro-F1} (or a confusion matrix), not raw accuracy -- that is where the improvement shows.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Exam Tips}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		\textbf{Likely to derive:}
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, nosep]
			\item \textbf{BPTT}: unroll, chain rule back through time. Recurrence Jacobian $\partial h_s/\partial h_{s-1}=W_h^\top g'(z_s)$; full gradient \emph{sums over steps}. The \textbf{product} $\prod W_h^\top g'(z_\tau)$ $\Rightarrow$ vanishing ($<1$) / exploding ($>1$).
			\item \textbf{LSTM super-highway}: from $c_t=f_t\odot c_{t-1}+i_t\odot\tilde c_t$ get $\partial c_t/\partial c_{t-1}=f_t$ and $\partial c_t/\partial c_s=\prod_\tau f_\tau$ $\Rightarrow$ explain why it fixes vanishing/exploding (learned gates, $f\!\approx\!1$ = identity path).
		\end{itemize}
		\textbf{Likely to compute:}
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, nosep]
			\item \textbf{Param counts} (independent of sequence length -- weight sharing!): SimpleRNN $n_h(n_h{+}n_x{+}1)$, \textbf{LSTM} $4\,n_h(n_h{+}n_x{+}1)$, \textbf{GRU} $3\,n_h(n_h{+}n_x{+}1)$, Dense head $n_{out}(n_h{+}1)$.
			\item \textbf{Tensor shapes} (\texttt{torch.nn.RNN}): \texttt{output} = all steps $(b,T,n_h)$; \texttt{hn} = last step only $(b,1,n_h)$.
		\end{itemize}
		\textbf{Likely to explain / compare:}
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, nosep]
			\item \textbf{Why RNN for sequences}: order matters, parameter sharing, arbitrary length. Sequential $\Rightarrow$ hard to parallelise over time (motivation for Transformers).
			\item \textbf{Sequence categories}: one-to-many, many-to-one (classification), many-to-many \emph{aligned} (NER, $n{:}n$) vs \emph{unaligned} (translation, needs seq2seq).
			\item \textbf{3 gates}: forget/input/output; $c_t$ = long-term, $h_t$ = short-term memory. \textbf{Forget gate} = key to long memory. \textbf{Multiplicative} gating is absent in MLP/CNN/vanilla RNN.
			\item \textbf{GRU vs LSTM}: GRU has no $c_t$, merges forget+input into the update gate $u_t$, adds reset gate $r_t$; 3 weight matrices vs 4 $\Rightarrow$ fewer params, similar accuracy.
			\item \textbf{Remedies for vanishing/exploding}: gradient clipping (exploding only), LSTM/GRU gating, good weight init.
			\item \texttt{return\_sequences}: \texttt{False} = last output (many-to-one); \texttt{True} = every step (many-to-many / stacking). Defaults: \texttt{activation=tanh}, \texttt{recurrent\_activation=sigmoid}.
		\end{itemize}
		\textbf{Common mistakes:}
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, nosep]
			\item Thinking gradient \textbf{clipping fixes vanishing} -- it only bounds \textbf{exploding}.
			\item Forgetting LSTM has \textbf{4$\times$} a vanilla RNN's parameters (and GRU 3$\times$).
			\item Confusing \texttt{output} vs \texttt{hn} shapes; confusing \emph{aligned} vs \emph{unaligned} many-to-many.
			\item Applying \textbf{batch-norm across time} (wrong -- distribution should change; risks exploding gradients); it belongs \emph{vertically} between stacked layers. Dropout must use the \textbf{same mask at every step}.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{examtipbox}
	
	% ==================================================
	\section{Generative Models -- Word Embeddings}
	% ==================================================

	\subsection{Generative Systems}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		A \textbf{generative system} generates new \emph{consistent} data from a seed. \textbf{Consistent} = respecting the temporal or spatial \textbf{structures} learned from the input space.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Deals with \textbf{sequential data}, related to:
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
			\item \textbf{Space} -- image
			\item \textbf{Time} -- speech, music
			\item \textbf{Sequence of symbols} -- text, DNA
		\end{itemize}
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Generating Sequential Data with RNNs}

	Generate one element at a time, feeding the prediction back as the next input (\textbf{autoregressive}). Two training schemes:

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Many-to-one}: read a sequence, output a single next element. To generate, append the prediction to the input and repeat (sliding window).
		\item \textbf{Many-to-many (shifted)}: target is the input sequence shifted by one; the net predicts the next element at \emph{every} timestep. More efficient -- one loss per step, full \textbf{teacher forcing} during training.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Shifted} = target$_t$ = input$_{t+1}$. The output sequence is the input delayed by one step.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Likely: \emph{``how do you generate a sequence with an RNN?''} $\rightarrow$ autoregressive loop, sample $\rightarrow$ feed back. Know the difference: many-to-one gives one prediction per pass; many-to-many (shifted) supervises every timestep and trains faster via teacher forcing.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Char-level Many-to-one with RNNs}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		Train the RNN to \textbf{predict the next character} in a many-to-one setting. At test time, an initial sequence (\textbf{seed}) is fed in and the next char is \textbf{sampled} from the softmax posterior. The output is appended to the seed and the process repeats (autoregressive).
	\end{definitionbox}

	\textbf{Pipeline} (operating at character level):
	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Encoding}: each char $\rightarrow$ \textbf{one-hot} vector with a $1$ at its position in the alphabet of size $D$ (e.g.\ \texttt{\{a,b,c,\dots,A,B,C,\dots,-,",\_,END\}}).
		\item \textbf{RNN}: feed $x_0,\dots,x_t$ through the recurrent cells; the last hidden state feeds a \textbf{softmax} over the $D$ symbols.
		\item \textbf{Sampling}: randomly pick the winner char \emph{respecting} the output distribution (not argmax).
		\item \textbf{Decoding}: map the chosen one-hot back to a character.
	\end{enumerate}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[
		font=\small,
		cell/.style={draw, fill=green!18, minimum size=8mm},
		op/.style={draw, fill=orange!20, minimum width=7mm, minimum height=7mm, align=center},
		sm/.style={draw, fill=green!18, minimum width=10mm, minimum height=6mm},
		>=stealth, node distance=10mm
	]
		% input chars
		\node (c0) at (0,0) {\texttt{d}};
		\node (c1) at (1.6,0) {\texttt{e}};
		\node (c2) at (3.2,0) {\texttt{e}};
		\node (c3) at (4.8,0) {\texttt{p}};
		% encoding bar
		\node[op, fill=orange!20, minimum width=5.2cm] (enc) at (2.4,0.9) {encoding (one-hot)};
		% rnn cells
		\node[cell] (a0) at (0,2.2) {$A$};
		\node[cell] (a1) at (1.6,2.2) {$A$};
		\node[cell] (a2) at (3.2,2.2) {$A$};
		\node[cell] (a3) at (4.8,2.2) {$A$};
		\draw[->] (a0)--(a1); \draw[->] (a1)--(a2); \draw[->] (a2)--(a3);
		\foreach \i in {0,1,2,3} { \draw[->] (enc.north -| a\i) -- (a\i.south); }
		\foreach \i in {0,1,2,3} { \draw[->] (c\i) -- (enc.south -| c\i); }
		% softmax + sampling + decoding
		\node[sm] (soft) at (6.7,2.2) {softmax};
		\node[op, minimum height=9mm] (samp) at (8.2,2.2) {samp-\\ling};
		\node[op, minimum height=9mm] (dec) at (9.7,2.2) {deco-\\ding};
		\node (out) at (11,2.2) {\texttt{e}};
		\draw[->] (a3)--(soft);
		\draw[->,dashed] (soft)--(samp);
		\draw[->,dashed] (samp)--(dec);
		\draw[->,dashed] (dec)--(out);
	\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Sample, don't argmax.} In generative mode we sample from the softmax probabilities $\Rightarrow$ \textbf{variability} in the output for similar inputs. Pure argmax would be deterministic and repetitive.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Be able to describe the full pipeline: char $\rightarrow$ one-hot ($D$ = alphabet size) $\rightarrow$ RNN $\rightarrow$ softmax $\rightarrow$ \textbf{sample} $\rightarrow$ decode. Common question: \emph{``why sample instead of taking the most likely char?''} $\rightarrow$ to introduce diversity / avoid deterministic loops.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Many-to-one in Keras (+ Parameter Counting)}

	Same architecture as name classification; here the output is $D=65$ chars (\texttt{num\_char}).

	\begin{verbatim}
model = keras.models.Sequential()
model.add(keras.layers.InputLayer(shape=(sentence_length, num_chars)))
model.add(keras.layers.SimpleRNN(256, return_sequences=False))
model.add(keras.layers.Dense(num_chars))
model.add(keras.layers.Activation('softmax'))
model.compile(loss='categorical_crossentropy', optimizer='adam',
              metrics=['accuracy'])
	\end{verbatim}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{InputLayer} \texttt{shape}: (\texttt{sentence\_length}, \texttt{num\_chars}) -- sequence length $\times$ element dim.
		\item \textbf{SimpleRNN} \texttt{units}: hidden-state size (here 256).
		\item \texttt{return\_sequences=False}: return \emph{only} the last hidden state (many-to-one), not the full sequence.
		\item \textbf{Dense + softmax}: classify the last state over the $D$ chars.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{@{}llr@{}}
		\toprule
		Layer & Output shape & Param \# \\
		\midrule
		\texttt{simple\_rnn} (SimpleRNN) & (None, 256) & 82\,432 \\
		\texttt{dense} (Dense) & (None, 65) & 16\,705 \\
		\texttt{activation} (softmax) & (None, 65) & 0 \\
		\midrule
		\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textbf{Total}} & \textbf{99\,137} \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{SimpleRNN} with $h$ units, input dim $D$:
		\[
		\#\text{params} = \underbrace{h\cdot h}_{W_h} + \underbrace{h\cdot D}_{W_x} + \underbrace{h}_{b_h} = h\,(h + D + 1)
		\]
		\[
		256\cdot256 + 256\cdot65 + 256 = 65536 + 16640 + 256 = 82\,432
		\]
		\textbf{Dense} with $D$ outputs from $h$ inputs:
		\[
		\#\text{params} = h\cdot D + D = 256\cdot65 + 65 = 16\,705
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $h$: hidden units (256) \quad $D$: alphabet / num chars (65)
		\item $W_h$: recurrent (state$\to$state) weights \quad $W_x$: input weights \quad $b_h$: hidden bias
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Classic \textbf{derive} question: ``how many parameters in this RNN?'' Don't forget the \textbf{recurrent} term $h\cdot h$ and the \textbf{bias} $h$ -- the most common mistakes. Full formula: $h(h+D+1)$ for SimpleRNN, $h\,D+D$ for the Dense head.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Building the Vocabulary}

	Get the sorted set of unique chars, then two lookup dicts for encoding/decoding:

	\begin{verbatim}
chars = sorted(list(set(corpus)))          # 65 unique chars
num_chars = len(chars)
encoding = {c: i for i, c in enumerate(chars)}   # char -> int
decoding = {i: c for i, c in enumerate(chars)}   # int  -> char
	\end{verbatim}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \texttt{encoding}: char $\rightarrow$ integer index (used before one-hot).
		\item \texttt{decoding}: integer index $\rightarrow$ char (used after sampling).
		\item \texttt{num\_chars} $= D = 65$ = alphabet size = one-hot / softmax dimension.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Preparing Inputs and Outputs}

	Slice the corpus into overlapping ``sentences'' with a \textbf{sliding window}: each $X$ is \texttt{sentence\_length} chars, the target $y$ is the \emph{next} char.

	\begin{verbatim}
sentence_length = 20      # input sequence length
skip = 1                  # stride between windows
