for i in range(0, len(corpus) - sentence_length, skip):
    sentence  = corpus[i : i + sentence_length]   # 20 chars
    next_char = corpus[i + sentence_length]       # 21st char = target
    X_data.append([encoding[c] for c in sentence])
    y_data.append(encoding[next_char])
	\end{verbatim}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \texttt{sentence\_length}: window length (20). \quad \texttt{skip}: stride over the corpus.
		\item $\approx \texttt{len(corpus)}/\texttt{skip}$ overlapping windows (here 1\,115\,374).
		\item Each char stored as its \textbf{integer index} (from \texttt{encoding}); one-hot done later.
		\item Example: $X[0]=[18,47,56,\dots]\to$ \texttt{['F','i','r','s','t',\dots]}, \; $y[0]\to$ \texttt{'e'}.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\texttt{skip} controls overlap/dataset size: \texttt{skip=1} = max overlap (most samples); larger \texttt{skip} = fewer, less-redundant windows. Window is the \textbf{context}; target is always the single following char (many-to-one).
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Vectorising to One-hot}

	Turn the integer indices into \textbf{one-hot} tensors (boolean) for $X$ and $y$:

	\begin{verbatim}
X = np.zeros((num_sentences, sentence_length, num_chars), dtype=bool)
y = np.zeros((num_sentences, num_chars), dtype=bool)
for i, sentence in enumerate(X_data):
    for t, encoded_char in enumerate(sentence):
        X[i, t, encoded_char] = 1     # set the one-hot bit
    y[i, y_data[i]] = 1
	\end{verbatim}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Tensor shapes} (sanity check):
		\[
		X:\ (\underbrace{1\,115\,374}_{\text{\#sentences}},\ \underbrace{20}_{\text{seq len}},\ \underbrace{65}_{\text{chars}}) \qquad
		y:\ (1\,115\,374,\ 65)
		\]
		$X$ is 3-D (one-hot per timestep); $y$ is 2-D (one one-hot target per sentence, many-to-one).
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Know the \textbf{shapes}: input $(N,\,L,\,D)$ = (samples, sequence length, vocab); many-to-one target $(N,\,D)$. Always \textbf{sanity-check tensor dimensions} before training. \texttt{dtype=bool} saves memory since one-hot is just 0/1.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Many-to-many Approach}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		In \textbf{many-to-many}, the RNN emits an output $y_t$ at \emph{every} timestep; a per-step loss $L_t$ is computed and the total loss sums over all steps. Contrast with many-to-one (single output/loss at the last step).
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[
		font=\small, >=stealth,
		h/.style={draw, fill=green!18, minimum size=6mm},
		f/.style={draw, fill=gray!22, minimum size=6mm},
		inp/.style={draw, fill=red!15, minimum size=6mm},
		outp/.style={draw, fill=blue!12, minimum size=6mm},
		l/.style={draw, fill=violet!15, minimum size=6mm}
	]
		% hidden chain
		\foreach \i/\xpos in {0/0,1/2,2/4,3/6} { \node[h] (h\i) at (\xpos,0) {$h_{\i}$}; }
		\node[h] (hT) at (8,0) {$h_T$};
		\foreach \i/\xpos in {1/1,2/3,3/5} { \node[f] (f\i) at (\xpos,0) {$f_W$}; }
		\draw[->] (h0)--(f1); \draw[->] (f1)--(h1); \draw[->] (h1)--(f2);
		\draw[->] (f2)--(h2); \draw[->] (h2)--(f3); \draw[->] (f3)--(h3);
		\draw[->,dashed] (h3)--(hT);
		% inputs
		\foreach \i/\xpos in {1/1,2/3,3/5} { \node[inp] (x\i) at (\xpos,-1.4) {$x_{\i}$}; \draw[->] (x\i)--(f\i); }
		% per-step outputs + losses
		\foreach \i/\xpos in {1/1,2/3,3/5} {
			\node[outp] (y\i) at (\xpos,1.4) {$y_{\i}$};
			\node[l] (L\i) at (\xpos+0.9,1.4) {$L_{\i}$};
			\draw[->] (h\i)--(y\i); \draw[->] (y\i)--(L\i);
		}
		\node[outp] (yT) at (8,1.4) {$y_T$};
		\node[l] (LT) at (8.9,1.4) {$L_T$};
		\draw[->] (hT)--(yT); \draw[->] (yT)--(LT);
		% total loss
		\node[l, fill=violet!25, minimum width=12mm] (tot) at (4.5,2.9) {Loss};
		\foreach \i in {1,2,3} { \draw[->] (L\i)--(tot); }
		\draw[->] (LT)--(tot);
	\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Difference is mainly at \textbf{training time}: cells emit an output at \emph{each} step.
		\item \textbf{Loss = sum over all step outputs} (vs.\ 1 output in many-to-one).
		\item Output layer is \textbf{``time distributed''} -- same Dense+softmax applied to every timestep.
		\item Shared weights $W$ ($f_W$) across all steps -- identical to many-to-one.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		\textbf{Compare} many-to-one vs many-to-many: same recurrence/weights, but many-to-many supervises \emph{every} step (richer gradient signal, faster training via teacher forcing) and needs a \textbf{TimeDistributed} output layer. Many-to-one only uses the last hidden state.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Output Distribution Through Time \& Truncated BPTT}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		The many-to-many approach needs a \textbf{distribution of the output through time} and a \textbf{truncated back-propagation through time}.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\textbf{1. ``A distribution of the output through time''}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item This is the \textbf{TimeDistributed} output layer: the same Dense + softmax head is applied at each of the $L$ steps -- the output layer is \emph{distributed across time}.
		\item Each timestep produces its own \textbf{probability distribution} (softmax over the $D$ chars); the loss is \textbf{summed over all per-step distributions}.
		\item I.e.\ \emph{one softmax output per timestep}, not just one at the end.
	\end{itemize}

	\textbf{2. ``A truncated back-propagation through time'' (TBPTT)}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Training an RNN = \textbf{unroll} it and backpropagate the loss through every timestep = \textbf{Backpropagation Through Time (BPTT)}.
		\item For long sequences BPTT is:
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
			\item \textbf{expensive} -- must store all activations for the whole sequence;
			\item \textbf{unstable} -- gradients vanish/explode over many steps.
		\end{itemize}
		\item \textbf{Truncated BPTT}: process the sequence in \textbf{chunks} and let the gradient flow back only a \textbf{limited number of steps} $k$, not through the whole sequence.
		\item Hidden state is still \textbf{carried forward} across chunks (forward pass keeps long context), but the \textbf{gradient is cut off} after $k$ steps.
		\item Many-to-many produces a loss at \emph{every} step $\Rightarrow$ per-chunk truncated training is natural.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Two things many-to-many needs: \textbf{(a)} a per-timestep softmax output (TimeDistributed); \textbf{(b)} \textbf{truncated} BPTT -- gradients trained step-by-step but only backpropagated a bounded distance $k$ to keep training tractable and stable. Know why we truncate: cost + vanishing/exploding gradients.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Many-to-many in Keras}

	Two changes vs many-to-one: \texttt{return\_sequences=True} and wrap Dense in \texttt{TimeDistributed}.

	\begin{verbatim}
model.add(keras.layers.InputLayer(shape=(sentence_length, num_chars)))
model.add(keras.layers.SimpleRNN(256, return_sequences=True))
model.add(keras.layers.TimeDistributed(
              keras.layers.Dense(num_chars, activation='softmax')))
	\end{verbatim}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \texttt{return\_sequences=True}: emit an output at \emph{every} step (not just the last).
		\item \texttt{TimeDistributed(Dense)}: apply the \emph{same} Dense+softmax to each timestep.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{@{}llr@{}}
		\toprule
		Layer & Output shape & Param \# \\
		\midrule
		\texttt{simple\_rnn} & (None, 100, 256) & 82\,432 \\
		\texttt{time\_distributed} (Dense) & (None, 100, 65) & 16\,705 \\
		\midrule
		\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textbf{Total}} & \textbf{99\,137} \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Same parameter count as many-to-one (99\,137)!} Weights are \textbf{shared across time}, so adding a time dimension changes the \emph{output shapes} (gain a length axis: \texttt{(None,\,100,\,$\cdot$)}) but \textbf{not} the number of parameters.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Trap question: ``does many-to-many have more parameters?'' $\rightarrow$ \textbf{No} -- weight sharing means identical params; only the output shape grows a time axis. Remember the two code changes: \texttt{return\_sequences=True} + \texttt{TimeDistributed}.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Finding -- faster convergence:} many-to-many reaches a \textbf{better loss in fewer epochs} than many-to-one. Reasonable, since training supervises \emph{every} step of the recurrence (more gradient signal per sequence) -- same parameters, richer training.
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Data Prep for Many-to-many ($y$ is shifted)}

	Now $y$ is a \textbf{sequence}, not a single char: take 20 chars \textbf{shifted by 1} as target.

	\begin{verbatim}
for i in range(0, len(corpus) - sentence_length, skip):
    sentence  = corpus[i : i + sentence_length]          # 20 chars
    next_char = corpus[i+1 : i+1 + sentence_length]      # 20 chars, shifted +1
    X_data.append([encoding[c] for c in sentence])
    y_data.append([encoding[c] for c in next_char])
# y one-hot loop now mirrors X (3-D):
for i, sentence in enumerate(y_data):
    for t, encoded_char in enumerate(sentence):
        y[i, t, encoded_char] = 1
	\end{verbatim}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \texttt{next\_char} = \texttt{corpus[i+1 : i+1+sentence\_length]} -- input shifted by one.
		\item Example: $X=$ \texttt{['F','i','r','s','t',\dots]}, \; $y=$ \texttt{['i','r','s','t',' ',\dots]}.
		\item $y$ is now the same shape as $X$: $(1\,115\,374,\ 20,\ 65)$ -- a one-hot \emph{per timestep}.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Many-to-one vs many-to-many target:} many-to-one $y$ = single next char, shape $(N,D)$. Many-to-many $y$ = next-char-at-every-step (input shifted $+1$), shape $(N,L,D)$ = same as $X$.
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Generating Text (Inference Loop)}

	One-hot the \textbf{seed}, then loop: predict $\rightarrow$ sample $\rightarrow$ append $\rightarrow$ slide window.

	\begin{verbatim}
X = np.zeros((1, sentence_length, num_chars), dtype=float)
for i, character in enumerate(seed_pattern):
    X[0, i, encoding[character]] = 1        # one-hot the seed

generated_text = ""
for i in range(500):
    output_prob = model.predict(X, verbose=0)[0][-1]   # last step, drop batch
    prediction  = np.random.choice(num_chars, p=output_prob)  # sample
    generated_text += decoding[prediction]
    activations = np.zeros((1, 1, num_chars), dtype=bool)
    activations[0, 0, prediction] = 1
    # drop first char, glue predicted one
    X = np.concatenate((X[:, 1:, :], activations), axis=1)
print(generated_text)
	\end{verbatim}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \texttt{predict(X)} returns shape $(1, 20, 65)$ = (batch, step, char-probs).
		\item \texttt{[0][-1]}: drop the batch dim and take the \textbf{last} step $\rightarrow$ vector of size 65.
		\item \texttt{np.random.choice(..., p=output\_prob)}: \textbf{sample} the winner char (not argmax).
		\item \texttt{concatenate((X[:,1:,:], activations))}: shift window -- drop oldest char, append new one.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		The window is \textbf{slid} each step: input keeps a fixed length \texttt{sentence\_length}, dropping the first char and appending the freshly generated one (autoregressive feedback). Sampling (not argmax) gives output variability.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Be able to explain the \textbf{generation loop}: seed $\rightarrow$ predict $\rightarrow$ sample from softmax $\rightarrow$ decode $\rightarrow$ shift window $\rightarrow$ repeat. Know why \texttt{[0][-1]} (last-step prediction, no batch) and why \texttt{np.random.choice} over \texttt{argmax} (diversity).
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{What to Expect \& How to Evaluate}

	\textbf{What can we expect out of the RNN?}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Make sense?} Locally yes (plausible short fragments), but \textbf{long-range coherence is weak} -- it captures statistical structure, not meaning.
		\item \textbf{Valid words?} Mostly -- a char-level RNN learns spelling and word boundaries -- but it can still emit \textbf{non-words} and ungrammatical sequences.
	\end{itemize}

	\textbf{How can we evaluate a generator?}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{At training time}: next-token prediction \textbf{accuracy} (also loss / perplexity).
		\item \textbf{At testing time}: \textbf{benchmarks} and reference-based metrics like \textbf{BLEU} (precision of $n$-gram overlap, translation) and \textbf{ROUGE} (recall of $n$-gram overlap, summarization).
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		Training accuracy measures next-char prediction, \emph{not} the quality of full generated sequences -- a model can have high training accuracy yet produce incoherent text. Generation quality needs test-time metrics.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Know the split: \textbf{train time} $\rightarrow$ accuracy/perplexity; \textbf{test time} $\rightarrow$ BLEU/ROUGE/benchmarks. Be ready to say \textbf{BLEU = precision-oriented} (translation), \textbf{ROUGE = recall-oriented} (summarization), and that no metric fully captures ``meaning''.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Why Word Embeddings?}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Network inputs must be \textbf{numerical, continuous} values $\Rightarrow$ words need a numeric encoding.
		\item Naïve choice: \textbf{1-hot (``atomic'') encoding} -- one dimension per vocabulary word, a single $1$ at the word's index, $0$ elsewhere.
		\item For a 50\,000-word vocabulary each word is a 50\,000-dim vector with a single $1$. \textbf{Generally does not work} as a classifier input.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{1-hot encoding is ``too sparse'':}
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em]
			\item Carries \emph{no information} beyond presence/absence of a word.
			\item No \emph{distribution} of relevant information across inputs (each dim = one word).
			\item No \emph{continuity}: all words are equidistant -- \texttt{apple} is as ``far'' from \texttt{orange} as from \texttt{car} (orthogonal, similarity $=0$).
		\end{itemize}
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{Goal of embeddings:} replace the long sparse 1-hot vector with a short \textbf{dense} vector whose geometry encodes meaning, so similar words end up close together.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\subsection{Word Embeddings}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		A \textbf{word embedding} is a projection of a word into a vector of real numbers -- a mapping where each word becomes a \textbf{coordinate} in the embedding space. Two families:
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em]
			\item \textbf{Sparse} embedding -- e.g.\ 1-hot encoding.
			\item \textbf{Dense} embedding -- e.g.\ word2vec, GloVe.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{definitionbox}

	\textbf{Example -- dense embedding into a 3-D space} (each row = one word's vector):
	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{l ccc}
		\toprule
		word & \multicolumn{3}{c}{embedding (dim 1--3)} \\
		\midrule
		\textit{anarchism}  & $0.5$  & $0.1$  & $-0.1$ \\
		\textit{originated} & $-0.5$ & $0.3$  & $0.9$  \\
		\textit{as}         & $0.3$  & $-0.5$ & $-0.3$ \\
		\textit{a}          & $0.7$  & $0.2$  & $-0.3$ \\
		\textit{term}       & $0.8$  & $0.1$  & $-0.1$ \\
		\textit{of}         & $0.4$  & $-0.6$ & $-0.1$ \\
		\textit{abuse}      & $0.7$  & $0.1$  & $-0.4$ \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\textbf{Example -- dense embedding into a 2-D space:} semantically related words cluster together.
	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.0, font=\footnotesize]
		\draw[->,gray] (-3.2,0) -- (3.2,0);
		\draw[->,gray] (0,-2.2) -- (0,2.2);
		% fruit cluster (upper left)
		\node at (-2.4,1.4) {apple};
		\node at (-2.0,1.0) {orange};
		\node at (-2.4,0.7) {banana};
		% food/drink cluster (upper right)
		\node at (1.6,1.6) {juice};
		\node at (0.6,1.0) {rice};
		\node at (2.4,0.8) {milk};
		% vehicle cluster (lower)
		\node at (-0.6,-1.2) {bus};
		\node at (-1.0,-1.6) {train};
		\node at (0.4,-1.5) {car};
	\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Wanted property of the projection:} expose ``good'' features that make the task easier:
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em]
			\item \textbf{Distributed representation} of concepts -- meaning is spread across many dimensions (not one dim = one word).
			\item \textbf{Continuity} -- words sharing similar concepts sit close together in the space.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Embedding Layer}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Input}: 1-hot encoding of the word in a vocabulary of size $V$ -- or just the \emph{index} of the ``hot'' entry.
		\item \textbf{Weight matrix} $\mathbf{W}$ of shape $V \times N$ (one row per word).
		\item \textbf{Output}: a single $N$-dim vector $\mathbf{h}$ = the embedding.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\[
		\mathbf{h} = \mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{W} \;:=\; \mathbf{v}_{w_I}
		\]
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em]
			\item $\mathbf{x}$: 1-hot input vector ($V$-dim), $1$ at index $I$.
			\item $\mathbf{W}$: $V\times N$ embedding matrix.
			\item $\mathbf{v}_{w_I}$: row $I$ of $\mathbf{W}$ = embedding of word $w_I$.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{formulabox}

	\begin{importantbox}
		Because $\mathbf{x}$ is 1-hot, the matrix product $\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{W}$ is just a \textbf{row lookup}: it selects the row of $\mathbf{W}$ at the hot index -- no real multiplication needed. \\
		\emph{Example:} if \texttt{orange} is the 2nd word ($I=2$), then $\mathbf{h}$ = row 2 of $\mathbf{W}$, e.g.\ $[0.3,\,0.1,\,-0.2,\dots]$.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\footnotesize,
		innode/.style={circle,draw,fill=yellow!25,minimum size=16pt,inner sep=0},
		outnode/.style={circle,draw,fill=green!25,minimum size=20pt,inner sep=0}]
		% input nodes
		\node[innode] (x1) at (0,3)    {$x_1$}; \node[left=3pt of x1] {$0$};
		\node[innode] (x2) at (0,1.8)  {$x_2$}; \node[left=3pt of x2] {$1$};
		\node         (xd) at (0,0.9)  {$\vdots$};
		\node[innode] (xv) at (0,0)    {$x_V$}; \node[left=3pt of xv] {$0$};
		% output nodes
		\node[outnode] (h1) at (4,2.7) {$+$}; \node[right=3pt of h1] {$h_1$};
		\node[outnode] (h2) at (4,1.5) {$+$}; \node[right=3pt of h2] {$h_2$};
		\node          (hd) at (4,0.8) {$\vdots$};
		\node[outnode] (hn) at (4,0.1) {$+$}; \node[right=3pt of hn] {$h_N$};
		% edges
		\foreach \i in {x1,x2,xv}{\foreach \j in {h1,h2,hn}{\draw[gray] (\i)--(\j);}}
		% labels
		\node[align=center] at (0,3.9) {\textbf{Input}\\$V$ dims};
		\node[align=center] at (2,3.9) {\textbf{Weights}\\$V\times N$};
		\node[align=center] at (4,3.9) {\textbf{Output}\\$N$ units};
	\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}

	\subsection{Use of Embedding Layers}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Analogy with CNNs for image recognition:} the embedding layer is the \emph{first} layer of an NLP network and performs a kind of \textbf{feature extraction for words}.
	\end{itemize}

	\textbf{3 strategies:}
	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Train from scratch} -- learn the embedding on a given dataset for a given task. Result is \emph{specific} to that dataset/task.
		\item \textbf{Use a pre-trained ``generic'' embedding} (word2vec, GloVe) and \textbf{freeze} the layer -- analogous to \textit{transfer learning}.
		\item \textbf{Do both} -- initialise with pre-trained weights, then \textbf{fine-tune} during training.
	\end{enumerate}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Likely question: \emph{``Compare the three ways to use an embedding layer.''} Map each to transfer-learning terms: (1) random init + full training, (2) frozen pre-trained features, (3) pre-trained init + fine-tuning. Trade-off: scratch needs lots of task data; frozen is data-efficient but generic; fine-tuning balances both.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Embedding Layer in Keras}

	Sentiment analysis on movie reviews (vocab = 5000 top words, embedding dim = 32):
\begin{verbatim}
top_words = 5000
embedding_vector_length = 32
model = Sequential()
model.add(Embedding(top_words, embedding_vector_length,
                    input_length=max_review_length))
model.add(LSTM(100))
model.add(Dense(1, activation='sigmoid'))
model.compile(loss='binary_crossentropy', optimizer='adam',
              metrics=['accuracy'])
\end{verbatim}

	\textbf{\texttt{Embedding} takes 3 arguments:}
	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, nosep]
		\item \textbf{input dim} = vocabulary size ($V$, here $5000$).
		\item \textbf{output dim} = embedding vector dimension ($N$, here $32$).
		\item \textbf{input length} = length of input sequences (here a review of $500$ words).
	\end{enumerate}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{llr}
		\toprule
		Layer & Output shape & Params \\
		\midrule
		Embedding & (None, 500, 32) & $5000\times 32 = 160000$ \\
		LSTM(100) & (None, 100)     & $4\cdot 100(100{+}32{+}1) = 53200$ \\
		Dense(1, sigmoid) & (None, 1) & $1(100{+}1) = 101$ \\
		\midrule
		\textbf{Total} & & \textbf{213\,301} \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Embedding params} $= V \times N$ (a lookup table, no bias). Here $5000\times 32 = 160\,000$. The output keeps the time axis: \texttt{(batch, seq\_len, N)}.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Examiner favourite: \emph{count the parameters}. Embedding $= V\cdot N$ (\textbf{no} $+1$ bias, unlike Dense/LSTM). Pre-trained word2vec/GloVe weights can be loaded instead of training from scratch.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Word2Vec -- Principles}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{word2vec} produces word embeddings with \textbf{contextual similarity}: words that share a common \emph{context} are placed close together in the embedding space.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Hypothesis 1 (distributional):} a word is represented by its \textbf{context} (the surrounding words). \\
		E.g.\ \texttt{apple} and \texttt{orange} both occur in ``I eat an \_\_\_ every day'' $\Rightarrow$ they end up close together.
		\item \textbf{Hypothesis 2 (bag-of-words):} the \textbf{order/position} of context words is \emph{disregarded}. A window is taken around the target word; words in the window are dropped into a ``bag'' regardless of order.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		The bag-of-words assumption means word2vec embeddings are \textbf{static} (one vector per word, position-independent). Transformers re-introduce order via \textbf{positional encoding}.
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Word2Vec -- Training Strategies}

	Three model variants, all building on the basic \textbf{one-word-context} idea:
	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{lll}
		\toprule
		Model & Idea & Multi-word extension \\
		\midrule
		One-word context & predict 1 word from 1 word & baseline (educational only) \\
		\textbf{Skip-gram} & predict \emph{context} from center word & multi-word at the \textbf{output} \\
		\textbf{CBOW} & predict \emph{center} word from context & multi-word at the \textbf{input} \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{One-word context}: not really used in practice -- mainly for explaining the mechanism.
		\item \textbf{Skip-gram}: input = 1 word, output = many context words.
		\item \textbf{CBOW} (Continuous Bag-of-Words): input = many context words, output = 1 center word.
		\item \textbf{Negative sampling} makes training tractable (avoids the full softmax over $V$).
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		Mnemonic: \textbf{Skip-gram} expands the \textbf{output} (one $\to$ many context); \textbf{CBOW} expands the \textbf{input} (many context $\to$ one). They are mirror images of each other.
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Word2Vec -- One-Word-Context Model}

	Architecture: $V \to N \to V$. Input 1-hot word $\to$ hidden (\textbf{linear}) $\to$ output (\textbf{softmax}) predicting the context word. Two weight matrices: $\mathbf{W}_{V\times N}$ (input) and $\mathbf{W}'_{N\times V}$ (output).

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{1. Hidden layer} (row lookup, linear activation):
		\[ \mathbf{h} = \mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{W} := \mathbf{v}_{w_I} \]
		\textbf{2. Output logit} for word $j$ (dot product with the $j$-th \emph{column} of $\mathbf{W}'$):
		\[ u_j = \mathbf{v}'^{\,\top}_{w_j}\cdot \mathbf{h} \]
		\textbf{3. Softmax} $\to$ probability of context word $w_j$ given input $w_I$:
		\[ p(w_j \mid w_I) = y_j = \frac{\exp(u_j)}{\sum_{j'=1}^{V}\exp(u_{j'})} \]
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em]
			\item $\mathbf{v}_{w_I}$: \textbf{input} vector = row $I$ of $\mathbf{W}$ (the embedding of $w_I$).
			\item $\mathbf{v}'_{w_j}$: \textbf{output} vector = column $j$ of $\mathbf{W}'$.
			\item $V$: vocabulary size, \; $N$: embedding dim.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{formulabox}

	\begin{importantbox}
		Each word has \textbf{two} vectors: an input vector (in $\mathbf{W}$) and an output vector (in $\mathbf{W}'$). The \textbf{softmax denominator} sums over all $V$ words $\Rightarrow$ \textbf{CPU-expensive} for large vocabularies. This is exactly what \textbf{negative sampling} / hierarchical softmax avoid.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Know the three-step forward pass (lookup $\to$ dot product $\to$ softmax) and \emph{why} the full softmax is the bottleneck (denominator over $V$). Online demo: \texttt{ronxin.github.io/wevi/}.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Word2Vec -- Skip-Gram Model}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Extension of one-word-context to \textbf{multi-word context at the output layer}.
		\item From a single input word, predict its \textbf{bag of $C$ context words} (e.g.\ from \texttt{apple} $\to$ \texttt{eat, an, every, day}).
		\item Same hidden lookup as before: $\mathbf{h} = \mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{W} := \mathbf{v}_{w_I}$.
		\item The output matrix $\mathbf{W}'_{N\times V}$ is \textbf{shared} across all $C$ output positions $\Rightarrow$ every context slot uses the same softmax; output size is $C\times V$.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		Skip-gram = \textbf{1 input $\to$ $C$ outputs}, with one shared $\mathbf{W}'$. Because $\mathbf{W}'$ is shared and order is ignored (bag-of-words), the $C$ predicted distributions are identical -- training pushes them toward the actual context words.
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Word2Vec -- CBOW (Continuous Bag of Words)}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Extension of one-word-context to \textbf{multi-word context at the input layer} (mirror of skip-gram).
		\item From a \textbf{bag of $C$ context words}, predict the single center word (e.g.\ \texttt{eat, an, every, day} $\to$ \texttt{apple}).
		\item The input matrix $\mathbf{W}_{V\times N}$ is \textbf{shared} across all $C$ input words; the hidden layer is their \textbf{average}.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{formulabox}
		Hidden = average of the context word vectors:
		\[
		\mathbf{h} = \frac{1}{C}\,\mathbf{W}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}_1 + \mathbf{x}_2 + \dots + \mathbf{x}_C)
		      = \frac{1}{C}\big(\mathbf{v}_{w_1} + \mathbf{v}_{w_2} + \dots + \mathbf{v}_{w_C}\big)^{\top}
		\]
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em]
			\item $C$: number of context words, \; $\mathbf{x}_c$: 1-hot of context word $c$.
			\item $\mathbf{v}_{w_c}$: input embedding (row of $\mathbf{W}$) of context word $c$.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{formulabox}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{CBOW vs Skip-gram} (mirror images):
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em, nosep]
			\item \textbf{CBOW}: $C$ inputs $\to$ 1 output; $\mathbf{h}$ = \emph{average} of context vectors. Faster, better on frequent words.
			\item \textbf{Skip-gram}: 1 input $\to$ $C$ outputs; shared $\mathbf{W}'$. Slower, better on rare words \& small corpora.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Word2Vec -- Application Question}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		\textbf{Q: Could we use word2vec for word prediction in a search-engine keyword completion?}

		\textbf{A: Not directly / not a good fit.} Keyword completion is \emph{next-word prediction}, which is inherently \textbf{order-dependent} (``new york \_\_\_'' $\to$ ``times'', ``weather''). word2vec rests on the \textbf{bag-of-words} assumption: it \emph{disregards word order and position}, so it cannot model the sequential dependency a completion task needs.
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em, nosep]
			\item word2vec is trained to learn \textbf{embeddings}, not to be a \textbf{language model}; its softmax outputs context-symmetric probabilities, not ordered continuations.
			\item CBOW averages context (order lost); skip-gram predicts a bag (order lost) -- neither captures ``what comes \emph{next}''.
			\item \textbf{Better tools}: a sequence model (RNN/LSTM, $n$-gram LM, or Transformer) that respects order.
			\item \textbf{But}: word2vec embeddings are still useful as the \emph{input representation} (embedding layer) feeding such a model.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Word2Vec -- Softmax Bottleneck \& Solutions}

	\begin{importantbox}
		The full softmax (and its derived update rules) must \textbf{iterate over the entire vocabulary} $V$ at every step. \textbf{Impractical} when $V$ and the training corpus are large -- which is almost always the case.
	\end{importantbox}

	\textbf{Two solutions:}
	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{p{0.42\linewidth} p{0.42\linewidth}}
		\toprule
		\textbf{Hierarchical Softmax} & \textbf{Negative Sampling} \\
		(Morin \& Bengio, 2005) & (Mikolov et al., 2013) \\
		\midrule
		Build a \textbf{binary tree} over the output layer; cost per update $\propto \log_2 V$ instead of $V$. &
		Update only a \textbf{small sample} of outputs (the true word + a few ``negative'' words) instead of all $V$. \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Know \emph{why} the softmax is the bottleneck (normaliser sums over all $V$) and \emph{name both fixes}: hierarchical softmax ($\log V$ via a binary tree) and negative sampling (subsample negatives). Negative sampling is the more common in practice.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Word2Vec -- Geometric Operations}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item word2vec does more than cluster related words: it preserves \textbf{``path meaning''} -- the \emph{vector difference} between two words encodes a consistent semantic relationship.
		\item The same offset is reused across many word pairs:
	\end{itemize}
	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{ll}
		\toprule
		Relationship & Parallel offset (example) \\
		\midrule
		Male--Female & $\mathrm{king}\!\to\!\mathrm{queen}$, $\mathrm{man}\!\to\!\mathrm{woman}$ \\
		Verb tense   & $\mathrm{walking}\!\to\!\mathrm{walked}$, $\mathrm{swimming}\!\to\!\mathrm{swam}$ \\
		Country--Capital & $\mathrm{Spain}\!\to\!\mathrm{Madrid}$, $\mathrm{Japan}\!\to\!\mathrm{Tokyo}$ \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{formulabox}
		Famous analogy property (king $-$ man $+$ woman $\approx$ queen):
		\[
		\mathbf{v}_{\text{woman}} + \big(\mathbf{v}_{\text{king}} - \mathbf{v}_{\text{man}}\big) \approx \mathbf{v}_{\text{queen}}
		\]
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em]
			\item $\mathbf{v}_{\text{king}}-\mathbf{v}_{\text{man}}$: the ``royalty'' offset, added to \texttt{woman}.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{formulabox}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Classic exam item: \emph{solve an analogy} via vector arithmetic, or \emph{explain why} embeddings allow it (consistent linear offsets for relationships). Nearest neighbour to $\mathbf{v}_{\text{king}}-\mathbf{v}_{\text{man}}+\mathbf{v}_{\text{woman}}$ is \texttt{queen}.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{GloVe -- Global Vectors for Word Representation}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{GloVe} learns embeddings from \textbf{global corpus statistics}: not a pure ANN but a \textbf{statistical / count-based} approach -- dimensionality reduction of the \textbf{co-occurrence matrix} of word pairs.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Factorises the global word \textbf{co-occurrence matrix} (how often two words appear together in context).
		\item Embeddings relate to the \textbf{probability that two words co-occur}.
		\item \textbf{word2vec and co-occurrence-based methods are actually related} -- both ultimately capture co-occurrence statistics (prediction-based vs.\ count-based views of the same signal).
		\item Source: \texttt{nlp.stanford.edu/projects/glove}.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		\textbf{word2vec vs GloVe}: word2vec is \emph{prediction-based} (local context windows, trained like a neural net); GloVe is \emph{count-based} (global co-occurrence matrix factorisation). Both yield dense static embeddings and are theoretically linked.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{FastText}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{FastText} (Facebook, 2016) extends word2vec by representing each word as a \textbf{bag of character $n$-grams (sub-words)} instead of an atomic token.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Words are broken into \textbf{sub-word $n$-grams}. E.g.\ tri-grams of \texttt{apple} $\to$ \texttt{app}, \texttt{ppl}, \texttt{ple}.
		\item A word's embedding = \textbf{sum of its $n$-gram embeddings}.
		\item After training we have embeddings for all $n$-grams of the training set (not just whole words).
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Main advantage: out-of-vocabulary (OOV) words can still be represented} -- their $n$-grams likely appeared in other training words, so an unseen word still gets a vector.
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em, nosep]
			\item Useful for \textbf{company names} (\texttt{creativeinfotech} lands near IT concepts).
			\item Useful for \textbf{compound-word languages} (e.g.\ German).
		\end{itemize}
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Likely question: \emph{``How does FastText handle OOV words / why is it better than word2vec for rare or compound words?''} Answer: sub-word $n$-grams shared across words $\Rightarrow$ unseen words composed from known $n$-grams.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{ELMo -- Contextual Embeddings}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{ELMo} produces \textbf{contextual} embeddings: the vector of a word depends on the \emph{whole sentence}, not a fixed lookup. The same word gets \emph{different} embeddings in different contexts.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Intuition}: meaning changes with context. ``The \textit{plane} took off'' vs.\ ``the \textit{plane} surface'' vs.\ ``\textit{plane} geometry'' -- \texttt{plane} should get different vectors.
		\item Unlike word2vec/GloVe (one \textbf{fixed} vector per word), ELMo reads the \textbf{entire sentence} before assigning each word's embedding.
		\item Built from a \textbf{deep bidirectional LSTM language model (biLM)}: forward + backward LSTMs; the embedding $T_i$ is a learned combination of the hidden states.
		\item The term ``word embedding'' gives way to \textbf{``language models''}.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Static vs Contextual:}
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em, nosep]
			\item word2vec / GloVe / FastText $\Rightarrow$ \textbf{static} (one vector per word, context-free).
			\item ELMo / Transformers (BERT, \dots) $\Rightarrow$ \textbf{contextual} (vector depends on the sentence) $\Rightarrow$ resolves \textbf{polysemy}.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Examiner favourite: \emph{``Why can't word2vec distinguish the senses of \textnormal{\texttt{plane}}?''} Because it is static/context-free. ELMo (deep biLSTM LM) solves this by conditioning each embedding on the full sentence.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Wrap-up -- Comparison of Embeddings}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Word embedding} = projection of a word into a vector of real numbers.
		\item \textbf{3 strategies} to use an embedding layer: train from scratch, frozen pre-trained, or pre-trained init + fine-tune.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{llp{0.42\linewidth}}
		\toprule
		Method & Type & Key idea \\
		\midrule
		\textbf{word2vec} & static & preserves contextual similarity via local context windows \\
		\textbf{GloVe}    & static & similar, but from global co-occurrence statistics \\
		\textbf{FastText} & static & sub-word $n$-grams $\Rightarrow$ handles \textbf{OOV} words \\
		\textbf{ELMo}     & contextual & uses the whole sentence before embedding each word \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{ELMo and Transformers} (BERT, \dots) were the foundation ideas for recent breakthrough language models -- the shift from \emph{static embeddings} to \emph{contextual language models}.
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Exam Tips}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Embeddings are \emph{static} (one vector per word) -- contrast with contextual embeddings from ELMo/Transformers. The embedding matrix is just a learnable lookup table of shape (vocab $\times$ dim).
	\end{examtipbox}
	
	% ==================================================
	\section{Non-Seq Architectures, Transfer Learning, Autoencoders, SSL}
	% ==================================================

	\subsection{Keras Models -- Sequential vs Functional}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{Sequential model}: a plain linear \emph{stack} of layers (one input $\to$ one output). \\
		\textbf{Functional API}: build models as a \emph{computational graph} of layers $\Rightarrow$ needed for any \textbf{non-sequential} architecture.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item The functional API enables:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item \textbf{Multiple paths} in the computation graph (incl.\ \textbf{layer sharing} -- same layer reused on several inputs).
			\item \textbf{Multiple inputs} and \textbf{multiple outputs}.
		\end{itemize}
		\item A layer is defined by: connection type, \# of units, with/without bias, activation type.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
		\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
			\toprule
			& \textbf{Sequential} & \textbf{Functional} \\
			\midrule
			Topology      & linear stack        & arbitrary DAG \\
			Inputs/Outputs & single / single    & multiple / multiple \\
			Layer sharing & no                  & yes \\
			Use case      & simple MLP/CNN      & branches, skip/residual, multi-task \\
			\bottomrule
		\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		``When do you \emph{need} the functional API?'' $\to$ whenever the graph is not a single chain: shared layers, residual/skip connections, or multi-input/multi-output (multi-task) models. A Sequential model cannot express these.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{RECAP: MLP with Sequential -- Parameter Counting}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Layers added one after another; the \textbf{last added layer = output layer}.
		\item Typical 2-layer classifier: \texttt{InputLayer(D)} $\to$ \texttt{Dense(H, relu)} $\to$ \texttt{Dense(K, softmax)}.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{formulabox}
		Trainable params of a \texttt{Dense} layer (weights $+$ bias):
		\[
		\#\text{params} = (n_{\text{in}} \times n_{\text{out}}) + n_{\text{out}}
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $n_{\text{in}}$: input features to the layer \quad $n_{\text{out}}$: units in the layer ($+n_{\text{out}}$ = one bias per unit).
		\item Example ($D=784$, $H=300$, $K=10$): layer 1 $=784\cdot300+300=235{,}500$; layer 2 $=300\cdot10+10=3{,}010$; total $=238{,}510$.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		\textbf{Examiner favorite:} ``How many parameters does this layer/model have?'' Don't forget the \textbf{bias term} ($+n_{\text{out}}$). Output shape \texttt{(None, H)}: \texttt{None} = variable batch size.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Functional API -- MLP Example}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Callable (pairwise) syntax}: each layer is called on the tensor it takes input from $\to$ \texttt{layer\_out = Layer(...)(prev\_tensor)}. The graph is wired explicitly, then a \texttt{Model(inputs, outputs)} ties the input/output tensors together.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \texttt{visible = Input(shape=(D,))} -- input tensor declared explicitly.
		\item \texttt{hidden1 = Dense(H,'relu')(visible)} \quad \texttt{output = Dense(K,'softmax')(hidden1)}.
		\item \texttt{model = Model(inputs=visible, outputs=output)}.
		\item Input \textbf{shape tuple has a hanging last comma} for 1-D input: \texttt{(784,)} -- leaves room for the (implicit) mini-batch dimension.
		\item Same architecture as Sequential $\Rightarrow$ \textbf{identical parameter count} (238{,}510); only the explicit \texttt{InputLayer} appears (0 params).
		\item \textbf{Why this works:} a Python object is \emph{callable} (usable as \texttt{obj()}) when its class defines \texttt{\_\_call\_\_}; a Keras \texttt{Layer} implements it, so \texttt{Layer(...)(tensor)} = build layer, then call it on the tensor.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Know the \textbf{three steps}: (1) \texttt{Input(...)}, (2) wire layers with callable syntax, (3) \texttt{Model(inputs, outputs)}. Pitfall: forgetting to call each layer on its predecessor, or writing \texttt{(784)} instead of \texttt{(784,)} (latter is a tuple).
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Multi-Path CNN (Functional API)}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{Idea:} run \textbf{different filter sizes at the same stage} on the \emph{same} input, then merge. Different kernel sizes capture patterns of different scales $\Rightarrow$ handles \textbf{close and far objects} in one image.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzpicture}[
			font=\footnotesize,
			node distance=5mm and 9mm,
			box/.style={draw, rounded corners, minimum height=6mm, inner sep=3pt, align=center},
			>={Stealth[round]}]
			\node[box] (in) {\texttt{Input} (28,28,1)};
			\node[box, below left=8mm and 4mm of in] (c1) {Conv2D $k{=}3$};
			\node[box, below right=8mm and 4mm of in] (c2) {Conv2D $k{=}6$};
			\node[box, below=of c1] (b1) {Dropout$\to$Pool$\to$Flatten};
			\node[box, below=of c2] (b2) {Dropout$\to$Pool$\to$Flatten};
			\node[box, below=14mm of in] (cat) {\texttt{concatenate}};
			\node[box, below=of cat] (h) {Dense(100, relu)};
			\node[box, below=of h] (out) {Dense(10, softmax)};
			\draw[->] (in) -- (c1);
			\draw[->] (in) -- (c2);
			\draw[->] (c1) -- (b1);
			\draw[->] (c2) -- (b2);
			\draw[->] (b1) -- (cat);
			\draw[->] (b2) -- (cat);
			\draw[->] (cat) -- (h);
			\draw[->] (h) -- (out);
		\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Both branches read the \textbf{same input tensor} \texttt{visible}: \texttt{conv1=...(visible)}, \texttt{conv2=...(visible)}.
		\item Each branch: \texttt{Conv2D $\to$ Dropout $\to$ MaxPooling2D $\to$ Flatten}.
		\item Merge: \texttt{merge = concatenate([flat1, flat2])} $\to$ Dense(100) interpretation $\to$ Dense(10, softmax).
		\item This topology is \textbf{impossible in Sequential} (a branch + merge) -- requires the functional API.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		``Why mix filter sizes at the same stage?'' $\to$ multi-scale feature capture. Know that \textbf{branches must flatten to compatible shapes before \texttt{concatenate}}, and that the merge node is what makes this non-sequential.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Multi-Feature CNN (features from different stages)}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{Idea:} mix features from \textbf{different depths/stages} of one CNN chain. Early stages capture low-level cues (texture, edges); later stages capture high-level shapes. Combining them helps fine discrimination (e.g.\ \emph{cat vs.\ leopard} via texture).
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item One deepening chain: \texttt{conv1$\to$pool1 $\to$ conv2$\to$pool2 $\to$ conv3$\to$pool3}.
		\item \textbf{Tap each stage}: \texttt{flat1=Flatten(pool1)}, \texttt{flat2=Flatten(pool2)}, \texttt{flat3=Flatten(pool3)}.
		\item Merge all depths: \texttt{concatenate([flat1, flat2, flat3])} $\to$ Dense(100) $\to$ Dense(10, softmax).
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
		\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
			\toprule
			& \textbf{Multi-path} & \textbf{Multi-feature} \\
			\midrule
			Branches feed from & same input        & successive stages (chain) \\
			Varies             & filter \emph{size} per branch & feature \emph{depth} tapped \\
			Captures           & multi-\emph{scale} & low- $+$ high-level features \\
			\bottomrule
		\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Don't confuse the two functional-API CNN patterns: \textbf{multi-path} = parallel branches with different kernel sizes on the same input; \textbf{multi-feature} = concatenate flattened outputs taken from \emph{different depths} of one chain. Both end in \texttt{concatenate} + Dense.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Multiple Inputs (merging modalities)}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{Idea:} feed \textbf{several independent inputs}, each through its own sub-network (tower), then \texttt{concatenate} the towers before the dense head. Used to \textbf{merge different modalities}.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Each input has its own \texttt{Input(...)} + CNN tower; towers are flattened and concatenated.
		\item Declared with a \textbf{list of inputs}: \texttt{Model(inputs=[in1, in2], outputs=output)}.
		\item Use cases: multiple images of the same object (e.g.\ frames from a movie), \textbf{stereo} speech recording, different acquisition-device parameters.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Distinguish from multi-path: here the branches have \textbf{separate inputs} (one per modality), not one shared input. Key syntax cue: \texttt{inputs=[...]} (a list).
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Famous Example: Inception Module \& GoogLeNet}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{Inception module}: a \emph{multi-path} block that runs \textbf{$1{\times}1$, $3{\times}3$, $5{\times}5$ convolutions and $3{\times}3$ max-pooling in parallel} on the same input, then \texttt{concatenate}s the outputs along the channel axis. \\
		\textbf{GoogLeNet} (Inception v1, ILSVRC'14 winner, 22 layers): a deep stack of Inception modules.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzpicture}[
			font=\footnotesize,
			node distance=4mm and 5mm,
			box/.style={draw, rounded corners, minimum height=5.5mm, inner sep=2.5pt, align=center},
			>={Stealth[round]}]
			\node[box] (in) {previous layer};
			\node[box, below=10mm of in] (b2) {$1{\times}1$};
			\node[box, left=of b2] (b1) {$1{\times}1$};
			\node[box, right=of b2] (b3) {$1{\times}1$};
			\node[box, right=of b3] (b4) {$3{\times}3$ pool};
			\node[box, below=of b2] (c2) {$3{\times}3$};
			\node[box, below=of b3] (c3) {$5{\times}5$};
			\node[box, below=of b4] (c4) {$1{\times}1$};
			\node[box, below=24mm of in] (cat) {\texttt{concatenate} (depth)};
			\draw[->] (in) -- (b1); \draw[->] (in) -- (b2); \draw[->] (in) -- (b3); \draw[->] (in) -- (b4);
			\draw[->] (b2) -- (c2); \draw[->] (b3) -- (c3); \draw[->] (b4) -- (c4);
			\draw[->] (b1) -- (cat); \draw[->] (c2) -- (cat); \draw[->] (c3) -- (cat); \draw[->] (c4) -- (cat);
		\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Why non-sequential?} Parallel conv/pool branches merged by \texttt{concatenate} = a multi-path graph (cannot be a plain stack).
		\item \textbf{$1{\times}1$ convolutions} = cheap dimensionality reduction (``bottleneck'') placed \emph{before} the $3{\times}3$/$5{\times}5$ convs $\Rightarrow$ slashes compute and parameters.
		\item Multi-scale: different kernel sizes capture patterns at several scales in one block.
		\item \textbf{GoogLeNet auxiliary classifiers}: 2 extra softmax \emph{outputs} branched off intermediate layers \emph{during training} $\Rightarrow$ a \textbf{multiple-output} graph; they fight vanishing gradients and are removed at inference.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Inception = canonical \textbf{multi-path} architecture; GoogLeNet also uses \textbf{multiple outputs} (aux classifiers) -- so it exercises \emph{both} reasons the functional API exists. Remember the role of \textbf{$1{\times}1$ convs} (dimensionality reduction), an examiner favorite.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{RECAP: Conv Layers Extract Features}

	\begin{importantbox}
		Filter parameters are \textbf{learned}, so a CNN learns \emph{not only to classify but also to extract features}. \\
		\textbf{Deep Learning $=$ representation learning $+$ downstream-task learning} -- feature extraction is learned end-to-end, unlike classical ML where features are hand-crafted.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Classical ML: separate hand-designed \emph{feature extraction} $\to$ then a model.
		\item Deep learning: raw data $\to$ learned features (representation) $\to$ task head, all trained jointly.
		\item \textbf{Transfer learning idea:} re-use the learned \emph{feature-extraction} part on a new task.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Representation Learning}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{Representation learning}: creating models that \emph{automatically extract relevant (often generic) features} of the input -- capturing underlying patterns \textbf{without manually designed features}.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item A DNN is a \textbf{two-stage} process: (1) learn useful \emph{representations}, (2) a simple transform into the target.
		\item Goal: map \textbf{high-dimensional complex input $\to$ low-dimensional} form capturing task-relevant properties.
		\item Example: a classifier CNN learns to turn \textbf{non-linearly-separable} images into a low-dim representation where classes become \textbf{linearly separable}.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{Transfer Learning}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{Transfer learning}: use knowledge learned on tasks with \textbf{lots of labelled data} to help settings where \textbf{little labelled data} is available -- concretely, \textbf{re-use the feature-extraction (``representation'') part} of a pre-trained network.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Reuse a backbone on which years of engineering / huge data were spent; \textbf{fine-tune} on the smaller target task. Saves data and compute.
		\item \textbf{Traditional ML}: generalise to unseen data from patterns in \emph{its own} training set.
		\item \textbf{Transfer learning}: \emph{kickstart} generalisation by starting from patterns learned for a \emph{different} task -- works best when tasks are in the \textbf{same domain} (not too different).
		\item Two regimes:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item \textbf{Feature extraction}: freeze backbone, train a new head.
			\item \textbf{Fine-tuning}: unfreeze some/all layers with a \emph{small} learning rate.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		``Why does transfer learning help with little data?'' $\to$ the costly representation is already learned; you only fit a small head. Use a \textbf{small LR} when fine-tuning so pre-trained weights aren't destroyed. Best when source/target share a \textbf{domain}.
	\end{examtipbox}
	
	\subsection{Transfer Learning -- Practical Recipe}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Golden rule:} \emph{the more target data you have, the more layers you can (re)train.} Early layers learn \textbf{generic} features (edges/textures) $\to$ keep frozen; later layers are \textbf{task-specific} $\to$ retrain first.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{center}
		\begin{tabular}{@{}p{2.3cm}p{5.5cm}p{6cm}@{}}
			\toprule
			\textbf{Scenario} & \textbf{What you do} & \textbf{Notes} \\
			\midrule
			Pre-trained model & Available model (e.g.\ trained on ImageNet) to reuse. & The starting point. \\
			Small dataset \newline (\textbf{feature extractor}) & \textbf{Freeze all} conv layers; plug in a \textbf{fresh classifier head} and train \emph{only} that. & Features are fixed; head can even be a non-NN classifier (e.g.\ SVM). \\
			Medium dataset \newline (\textbf{fine-tuning}) & Freeze \emph{early} layers; \textbf{retrain later conv blocks $+$ head}. More data $\Rightarrow$ unfreeze more (up to all). & Often \textbf{2 phases}: (1) train the new head, (2) fine-tune the rest with a \textbf{small LR}. \\
			\bottomrule
		\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Likely Q: ``You have a small/medium dataset -- what do you freeze and train?'' Small $\to$ freeze backbone, train head only. Medium $\to$ also fine-tune upper layers with small LR. Why two phases? Training a random head with high gradients would otherwise wreck the pre-trained weights.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Autoencoders -- Definition}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		An \textbf{autoencoder} is a neural network trained to \textbf{reproduce its own input}, thereby discovering ``structures'' and \textbf{efficient codings} of the input space. A class of \emph{representation learning}.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Simplest form: a feedforward (non-recurrent) net like an MLP, with \textbf{output dimension $=$ input dimension}.
		\item Trained so the mapping $h_\theta(\mathbf{x}) = \hat{\mathbf{x}} \approx \mathbf{x}$.
		\item \textbf{No label targets} -- the teaching signal is the \emph{data itself} $\Rightarrow$ a form of \textbf{self-supervised learning}.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\[
		\mathbf{z} = E_{\theta_E}(\mathbf{x}), \qquad
		\hat{\mathbf{x}} = D_{\theta_D}(\mathbf{z}), \qquad
		\hat{\mathbf{x}} = D_{\theta_D}\!\big(E_{\theta_E}(\mathbf{x})\big) = h_\theta(\mathbf{x})
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $E_{\theta_E}$: encoder \quad $D_{\theta_D}$: decoder \quad $\mathbf{z}$: latent \textbf{feature / representation} vector.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{Loss = MSE quadratic reconstruction error} (input vs.\ reconstructed input):
		\[
		J(\theta) = J(\theta_E,\theta_D) = \frac{1}{2N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\big(h_\theta(\mathbf{x}_n) - \mathbf{x}_n\big)^2
		= \frac{1}{2N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\big(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_n - \mathbf{x}_n\big)^2
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Trained like any net by \textbf{gradient descent}: \emph{full} (all $N$/iter), \emph{stochastic} (1/iter), \emph{batch} ($b$/iter).
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzpicture}[
			font=\footnotesize, >={Stealth[round]},
			n/.style={circle, draw, minimum size=5mm, inner sep=0pt},
			every node/.style={}]
			% input layer (4)
			\foreach \i in {1,...,4} \node[n] (x\i) at (0,{4-\i}) {};
			% latent (2)
			\foreach \i in {1,2} \node[n, fill=black!8] (z\i) at (2.6,{2.5-\i}) {};
			% output layer (4)
			\foreach \i in {1,...,4} \node[n] (y\i) at (5.2,{4-\i}) {};
			% encoder connections
			\foreach \i in {1,...,4} \foreach \j in {1,2} \draw[->,gray] (x\i) -- (z\j);
			% decoder connections
			\foreach \j in {1,2} \foreach \i in {1,...,4} \draw[->,gray] (z\j) -- (y\i);
			\node[above=1mm] at (0,3) {$\mathbf{x}$};
			\node[above=1mm] at (2.6,1.7) {$\mathbf{z}$};
			\node[above=1mm] at (5.2,3) {$\hat{\mathbf{x}}$};
			\node at (1.3,-1.4) {\small Encoding $E_{\theta_E}$, $W_l$};
			\node at (3.9,-1.4) {\small Decoding $D_{\theta_D}$, $W_l'$};
		\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}

	\begin{importantbox}
		Architecture must \textbf{prevent learning a trivial copy} (identity). Two ways:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item \textbf{Bottleneck} -- hidden layer \emph{smaller} than input (``\textbf{diabolo}'' / undercomplete net).
			\item \textbf{More} hidden nodes but with \textbf{dropout} (regularised / overcomplete).
		\end{itemize}
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item After training, \textbf{cut the net in two}: keep the \emph{encoder} to produce representations $\mathbf{z}$; the decoder reconstructs.
		\item Variants: denoising, sparse, contractive autoencoders. Uses: dimensionality reduction, anomaly detection, pretraining.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		``Is an autoencoder supervised or unsupervised?'' $\to$ \emph{self-supervised}: no external labels, but the data is its own target. ``Why not just learn the identity?'' $\to$ the bottleneck (diabolo) or dropout forces it to compress. Know the encoder/decoder split and that $\mathbf{z}$ is the learned representation.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Autoencoders -- Keras Example (shallow dense)}

	\begin{verbatim}
encoding_dim = 32                                    # size of code z

input_img = Input(shape=(784,))                      # e.g. 28x28 flattened
encoded   = Dense(encoding_dim, activation='relu')(input_img)
decoded   = Dense(784, activation='sigmoid')(encoded)

autoencoder = Model(inputs=input_img, outputs=decoded)   # full AE: x -> x_hat
encoder     = Model(inputs=input_img, outputs=encoded)   # encoder: x -> z

# build a standalone decoder: z -> x_hat
encoded_input = Input(shape=(encoding_dim,))
decoder_layer = autoencoder.layers[-1]               # reuse trained last layer
decoder = Model(inputs=encoded_input,
                outputs=decoder_layer(encoded_input))
	\end{verbatim}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Compression ratio} $784 \to 32 \approx 24.5\times$; the bottleneck is what forces a compact code.
		\item \textbf{Encoder} \texttt{relu} (free internal code), \textbf{decoder} \texttt{sigmoid} (outputs in $[0,1]$ to match normalised pixels).
		\item Define the AE \emph{once}, then carve out \texttt{encoder} and \texttt{decoder} as sub-models sharing the \textbf{same trained weights} -- no retraining.
		\item \texttt{layers[-1]} reuses the trained decoder layer; the standalone decoder lets you decode arbitrary $\mathbf{z}$.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		``Why \texttt{sigmoid} on the output but \texttt{relu} on the code?'' $\to$ output must match the input range ($[0,1]$ pixels); the code has no such constraint. ``Why three \texttt{Model}s?'' $\to$ one to \emph{train} (autoencoder), two to \emph{reuse} the learned pieces (encoder for features, decoder for generation) -- all weight-shared.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Autoencoders -- Usages}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Usage 1 -- Data compression / dimensionality reduction.} Train an AE on all data; send the \textbf{decoder weights $W_l'$} to the receiver once. Then \textbf{encode}: transmit only the lower-dim $\mathbf{z}$ (instead of $\mathbf{x}$); the receiver \textbf{decodes} $\hat{\mathbf{x}} = D(\mathbf{z})$.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Link to PCA:} with \emph{linear} activations (or a single sigmoid hidden layer), the optimal AE solution relates to \textbf{Principal Component Analysis}.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Usage 2 -- Encoder as feature extractor.} Train a (conv-)AE on your data \emph{including unlabelled} samples; keep the \textbf{encoder} to compute $\mathbf{z}$; use $\mathbf{z}$ as features to train a classifier on the \textbf{labelled} part. (Semi-supervised: leverages unlabelled data.)
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Usage 3 -- Denoising autoencoder (DAE).} \textbf{Train:} feed a \emph{corrupted} input $\tilde{\mathbf{x}} = \mathbf{x} + \text{noise}$ but reconstruct the \emph{clean} target $\mathbf{x}$. \textbf{Test:} pass a noisy image $\to$ get a cleaner version. Key twist: the target is the \textbf{clean} signal, not the noisy input.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Why it helps:} forcing clean-from-noisy stops the net learning a trivial identity and pushes $\mathbf{z}$ to capture \textbf{robust structure}, not noise.
		\item \textbf{As a feature extractor:} learned features show \textbf{robustness to noise}.
		\item \textbf{Bonus:} injecting artificial noise is a form of \textbf{data augmentation}.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Usage 4 -- Image in-painting.} Goal: \textbf{restore lost information} by ``painting'' a missing region back in. The AE is trained to reconstruct the \emph{removed} patch from its surroundings.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Context encoders:} to fill a hole the encoder must learn features that capture not just \textbf{appearance} but also the \textbf{semantics} of visual structures $\to$ \textbf{robust, high-level features}.
		\item Like the DAE, the corruption (here: masking a region) is what forces a richer representation than a plain reconstruction AE.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Usage 5 -- Initialise a deep net for supervised learning} (greedy pre-training). Use cheap \textbf{unlabelled} data to find good starting weights, then refine with scarce \textbf{labelled} data.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Pre-train (unsupervised):} train a \textbf{stacked autoencoder} on unlabelled data.
		\item \textbf{Fine-tune step 1:} keep the \textbf{encoder}, add a fresh dense (output) layer, train \emph{only that head} on labelled data.
		\item \textbf{Fine-tune step 2:} if enough labelled data, \textbf{back-propagate through the whole network} to fine-tune end-to-end.
		\item Shown to \textbf{improve deep nets} -- historically how very deep models were made trainable before good init/normalisation.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Usage 6 -- Anomaly detection.} Train an AE on \textbf{only ``normal'' samples} (input $=$ output $=$ a non-damaged part). At test time, \textbf{reconstruction error} flags anomalies: defects were never seen, so they reconstruct \emph{poorly}.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Why it works:} the AE only learns to rebuild ``normal'' $\to$ high error on out-of-distribution (defective) inputs.
		\item \textbf{Recipe:} collect the \emph{distribution} of reconstruction errors on good parts; a defective part gives an error \textbf{outside} that distribution $\to$ threshold to decide.
		\item \textbf{Fits unbalanced data:} normal samples are plentiful, defects rare -- you train on the majority class only, no defect labels needed.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		``How is an autoencoder related to PCA?'' $\to$ linear AE $\approx$ PCA (learns the principal subspace). Usage 2 is the bridge to \textbf{transfer/self-supervised learning}: pretrain a representation on unlabelled data, then fit a small supervised head.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Transfer Learning vs Autoencoder}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		Both produce a \textbf{feature extractor} to reuse on a target task, but they learn it differently: transfer learning reuses a \textbf{discriminative} (supervised, label-driven) representation; an autoencoder learns a \textbf{generative/reconstructive} (unsupervised) representation.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Feature quality (expected):} transfer-learning features are usually \emph{more discriminative} -- they were tuned to separate classes -- \textbf{provided source and target share a domain}. AE features are tuned to \emph{reconstruct} the input, so they encode \textbf{everything} (incl.\ task-irrelevant detail) and are typically \emph{less discriminative} for a specific classifier.
		\item \textbf{When AE wins:} no suitable pre-trained model exists, or the domain is too different from any source -- then in-domain AE features beat mismatched transferred ones.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
		\toprule
		 & \textbf{Transfer learning} & \textbf{Autoencoder} \\
		\midrule
		Supervision (source) & needs \textbf{labelled} source data & \textbf{unlabelled} (self-supervised) \\
		Feature type & discriminative, task-driven & reconstructive, generic \\
		Discriminative power & high (if domains match) & lower (encodes all input info) \\
		Domain match & \textbf{critical} -- degrades if far & uses \textbf{your own} data, in-domain \\
		Main risk & negative transfer / domain gap & features not tuned to the task \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		``Quality of features: transfer learning vs autoencoder?'' $\to$ transfer learning $=$ better, \emph{more discriminative} features \textbf{when the source domain matches}; autoencoder $=$ no labels needed and always in-domain, but features are generic/reconstructive and less class-specific. \textbf{Trade-off:} availability of a matching pre-trained model vs.\ availability of (unlabelled) in-domain data.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Self-Supervised Learning (SSL) -- Definition}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{Self-Supervised Learning} learns useful \textbf{feature representations from unlabelled data}: a \textbf{pretext task} pre-trains a model, which is then \textbf{fine-tuned} on downstream tasks. Used in \textbf{NLP, vision and audio}.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Intermediate form} between supervised and unsupervised learning -- imitates how \emph{humans} learn to classify objects.
		\item Yields \textbf{pre-trained models} with a \emph{generic} data representation, reusable across downstream tasks.
		\item \textbf{Step 1 -- Pretraining:} profits from \textbf{large unlabelled} datasets (learns the representation via the pretext task).
		\item \textbf{Step 2 -- Fine-tuning:} done with \textbf{smaller labelled} datasets (adapts to the target task).
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{SSL} creates supervision from the data itself via \emph{pretext tasks} (no human labels), then transfers the learned representation. Examples: masked prediction, \textbf{contrastive learning} (SimCLR), pulling augmented views of the same image together and pushing others apart.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		``Is SSL supervised or unsupervised?'' $\to$ \textbf{intermediate}: labels are \emph{generated from the data} (pretext task), not given by humans. Know the \textbf{2 steps}: pretrain on large \emph{unlabelled} data $\to$ fine-tune on small \emph{labelled} data. Contrast with an autoencoder (reconstruction is one possible pretext task).
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{SSL -- Intuitive Example (BERT)}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{BERT} is trained by SSL with \textbf{2 pretext tasks}: (1) \textbf{predict masked words} (Masked LM); (2) \textbf{next-sentence prediction} -- is sentence B the one that follows A?
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Pseudo-labels:} the masked-out words \emph{are} the labels -- derived for free from the \textbf{unlabelled} text, no human annotation.
		\item \textbf{Downstream task:} fine-tune the pre-trained model on a labelled task, e.g.\ \emph{sentiment analysis}.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
		\toprule
		\textbf{Stage} & \textbf{Sentence} \\
		\midrule
		Original (unlabelled) & A quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog. \\
		Randomly masked       & A quick \texttt{[MASK]} fox jumps over the \texttt{[MASK]} dog. \\
		Prediction            & A quick \textbf{brown} fox jumps over the \emph{nice} dog. \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item The \texttt{[MASK]} positions' true words (\emph{brown}, \emph{lazy}) act as pseudo-labels; the model is trained to recover them (1st guess right, 2nd wrong).
	\end{itemize}

	\subsection{SSL -- SimCLR}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{SimCLR} (\emph{Simple Framework for Contrastive Learning of Visual Representations}, Google Research) learns image features by \textbf{contrastive learning}: pull two augmentations of the \emph{same} image together (\textbf{positives}) and push \emph{other} images apart (\textbf{negatives}).
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Step 1 -- Pre-training (unlabelled):}
		\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
			\item Augment the source image \textbf{twice} (two views).
			\item Both views pass the \textbf{encoder} $\to$ a 2000-dim representation each.
			\item Pass through a \textbf{projection head} (further encodes the representation).
			\item Optimise the \textbf{NT-Xent} loss: same-image pair $=$ positive, other in-batch samples $=$ negatives.
		\end{enumerate}
		\textbf{Step 2 -- Fine-tuning:}
		\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep,start=5]
			\item Reuse the \textbf{pre-trained encoder} (drop the projection head) and fine-tune on downstream tasks.
		\end{enumerate}
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Shared weights:} the two branches are the \emph{same} encoder $f(\cdot)$ (and same head $g(\cdot)$) applied to both views -- a \textbf{Siamese} network, \emph{not} two independent encoders.
		\item \textbf{Negatives come from the batch} $\to$ contrastive methods like SimCLR benefit from \textbf{large batch sizes} (more negatives per step) and \textbf{long training} (many epochs). Cost: needs \textbf{GPUs with lots of memory}.
		\item \textbf{Projection head} used only during pre-training; \textbf{discarded} for downstream use.
		\item \emph{SimCLR v2:} larger network + larger projection head.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[
		font=\footnotesize,
		>=stealth,
		circ/.style={draw, circle, inner sep=1.5pt},
		every edge/.style={draw, ->}]
		% nodes: left branch (i), right branch (j)
		\node[circ] (x)  at (2,0)   {$\mathbf{x}$};
		\node[circ] (xi) at (0,1.4) {$\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_i$};
		\node[circ] (xj) at (4,1.4) {$\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_j$};
		\node (hi) at (0,3.0) {$\mathbf{h}_i$};
		\node (hj) at (4,3.0) {$\mathbf{h}_j$};
		\node (zi) at (0,4.6) {$\mathbf{z}_i$};
		\node (zj) at (4,4.6) {$\mathbf{z}_j$};
		% augmentation (bottom)
		\draw (x) edge node[below left=-1pt, font=\scriptsize]{$t\sim\mathcal{T}$} (xi);
		\draw (x) edge node[below right=-1pt, font=\scriptsize]{$t'\sim\mathcal{T}$} (xj);
		% encoder f
		\draw (xi) edge node[left, font=\scriptsize]{$f(\cdot)$} (hi);
		\draw (xj) edge node[right, font=\scriptsize]{$f(\cdot)$} (hj);
		% projection head g
		\draw (hi) edge node[left, font=\scriptsize]{$g(\cdot)$} (zi);
		\draw (hj) edge node[right, font=\scriptsize]{$g(\cdot)$} (zj);
		% horizontal annotations
		\draw[<->] (zi) -- node[above, font=\scriptsize]{Maximise agreement} (zj);
		\draw[<->] (hi) -- node[above, font=\scriptsize]{Representation} (hj);
	\end{tikzpicture}
	\\[2pt]
	{\footnotesize Both branches use the \textbf{same shared-weight} encoder $f(\cdot)$ (\textbf{ResNet-50}) and projection head $g(\cdot)$ -- it is \emph{one} network applied twice (Siamese), not two. The $\mathbf{h}$ representation is reused downstream; $g(\cdot)$ is discarded.}
	\end{center}

	\begin{formulabox}
		Per-pair NT-Xent (Normalised Temperature-scaled Cross-Entropy) loss:
		\[ \ell(i,j) = -\log \frac{\exp(s_{i,j}/\tau)}{\sum_{k=1}^{2N} \mathbb{1}_{[k\neq i]}\,\exp(s_{i,k}/\tau)} \]
		Total loss over a batch of $N$ images ($2N$ views):
		\[ \mathcal{L} = \frac{1}{2N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\big[\ell(2k-1,2k)+\ell(2k,2k-1)\big] \]
	\end{formulabox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $s_{i,j}$: similarity (cosine) between projected views $\mathbf{z}_i,\mathbf{z}_j$.
		\item $\tau$: temperature (scales the logits; smaller $\to$ sharper).
		\item $N$: batch size; $2N$: total augmented views; $\mathbb{1}_{[k\neq i]}$: excludes the sample itself from the denominator.
		\item Numerator $=$ the \textbf{positive} pair; denominator sums over \emph{all other} ($2N-1$) views $=$ the \textbf{negatives}. $\mathcal{L}$ averages the loss \textbf{both directions} of each pair.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		``BERT vs SimCLR?'' $\to$ both SSL, but BERT $=$ \textbf{predictive/generative} pretext (masked words), SimCLR $=$ \textbf{contrastive} (positives vs negatives, NT-Xent). ``Why a big batch?'' $\to$ negatives are the other batch samples. ``Why drop the projection head?'' $\to$ the \emph{encoder} representation transfers better than the projected one.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Wrap-up}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Keras functional API:} build \emph{non-sequential} architectures -- multiple inputs/outputs, shared/branched feature extraction.
		\item \textbf{Transfer learning:} export knowledge to a new environment by \textbf{re-using feature-extraction layers} of a pre-trained net $\to$ enables deep learning on \textbf{small datasets}.
		\item \textbf{Autoencoders:} nets trained to \textbf{reproduce their input}, discovering a useful internal representation. \textbf{Diabolo} topology; forms: shallow dense, stacked dense, CNN. \textbf{6 usages:} compression, feature extraction, de-noising, image in-painting, deep-net pre-training, anomaly detection.
		\item \textbf{Self-supervised learning:} learn from \textbf{large unlabelled} data via a \textbf{pretext task}; like transfer learning, fine-tune on a \textbf{downstream task}.
		\item \textbf{Representation / feature learning:} techniques to \emph{discover} the representations needed for feature extraction from raw data -- \textbf{replaces manual feature engineering}.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		Common thread: all four learn a \textbf{reusable representation} so a downstream task needs less labelled data. They differ in the \emph{source} of the learning signal: \textbf{labels} (transfer), \textbf{reconstruction} (autoencoder), or a \textbf{pretext task} (SSL).
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Exam Tips}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		Transfer learning shines with little target data. Autoencoders are unsupervised reconstruction; SSL uses pretext tasks to learn general features. Contrastive losses need negatives (or tricks like BYOL to avoid them).
	\end{examtipbox}

	% ==================================================
	\section{Seq2Seq -- Attention -- Transformers}
	% ==================================================
	
	\subsection{Seq2Seq}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		A \textbf{sequence-to-sequence (Seq2Seq)} model maps an input sequence to an output sequence of \emph{possibly different length}, where the \textbf{entire input must be read before output prediction can start}.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Applications}: machine translation, question-answering bots, grapheme-to-phoneme mapping.
		\item Initial architectures are \textbf{RNN-based}: two RNNs -- an \textbf{encoder} and a \textbf{decoder}.
		\item \textbf{Encoder}: reads the whole input and compresses it into a fixed-size \textbf{context vector} $h_t$ (its final hidden state) -- a numerical summary of the input's \emph{meaning}. Called a \textbf{sentence/thought embedding} when inputs are words.
		\item \textbf{Decoder}: initialised with the context vector, generates the output \textbf{token by token} (autoregressively, feeding each output back as the next input). Original Seq2Seq used \emph{only the encoder's last state} as the decoder's initial state.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=8mm, font=\small,
		enc/.style={draw, fill=blue!10, minimum size=7mm},
		dec/.style={draw, fill=green!10, minimum size=7mm}]
		\node[enc] (e1) {$h_1$};
		\node[enc, right=of e1] (e2) {$h_2$};
		\node[enc, right=of e2] (e3) {$h_3$};
		\node[dec, right=14mm of e3] (d1) {$s_1$};
		\node[dec, right=of d1] (d2) {$s_2$};
		% encoder inputs
		\node[below=5mm of e1] (x1) {$x_1$};
		\node[below=5mm of e2] (x2) {$x_2$};
		\node[below=5mm of e3] (x3) {$x_3$};
		% decoder outputs
		\node[above=5mm of d1] (y1) {$y_1$};
		\node[above=5mm of d2] (y2) {$y_2$};
		\draw[->] (x1)--(e1); \draw[->] (x2)--(e2); \draw[->] (x3)--(e3);
		\draw[->] (e1)--(e2); \draw[->] (e2)--(e3);
		\draw[->] (d1)--(d2);
		\draw[->] (d1)--(y1); \draw[->] (d2)--(y2);
		\draw[->, thick] (e3)-- node[above, font=\scriptsize]{context} (d1);
	\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}

	\textbf{Layered structure} (each token, both sides): one-hot vector $\to$ \textbf{embedding layer} $\to$ \textbf{recurrent layer}; the decoder adds an \textbf{output layer} (softmax over vocabulary).

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{Notation} ($s$ = source/encoder, $t$ = target/decoder):
		\[
		\tilde{x}_i,\ h_i^{(s)} \quad\text{(enc)}\qquad
		z = h_0^{(t)} = h_N^{(s)} \quad\text{(context)}\qquad
		\tilde{y}_j,\ h_j^{(t)},\ o_j \quad\text{(dec)}
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $x_i$: one-hot input, $\tilde{x}_i$: its embedding; $o_j$: decoder output (softmax) at step $j$.
		\item Decoder is fed the \emph{previous} token $y_{j-1}$ to predict $y_j$ (autoregressive / teacher forcing).
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		Special tokens \textbf{\texttt{<BOS>}} (beginning) and \textbf{\texttt{<EOS>}} (end of sentence): \texttt{<BOS>} kicks off decoding; generation stops when the decoder emits \texttt{<EOS>}.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{importantbox}
		The single fixed-size \textbf{context vector} is a bottleneck: all input information must be squeezed into it, which degrades for long sequences -- solved by \textbf{attention}.
	\end{importantbox}
	
	\subsection{Attention}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{Attention} is an \textbf{interface between encoder and decoder} that gives the decoder information from \emph{all (or parts of)} the encoder hidden states -- not just the final one $h_t$.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Without attention, the decoder receives \textbf{only} the last hidden state $h_t$ (the bottleneck).
		\item \textbf{Intuition = alignment}: which input segments match which output segments, i.e.\ \emph{where the decoder should focus} to produce the current output token.
		\item \textbf{Global attention}: uses \emph{all} encoder hidden states.
		\item \textbf{Local attention}: uses only a \emph{subset} of the encoder hidden states.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
		\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{images_summary/seq2seq_attention_layer.png}
	\end{center}

	\textbf{Attention steps} (one context vector per decoder step):
	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, label=\textbf{\arabic*.}, start=0]
		\item \textbf{Prepare hidden states}: take all encoder hidden states (green) and the current decoder hidden state (red).
		\item \textbf{Score each encoder state}: a \textbf{score function} (a.k.a.\ \emph{alignment score function / alignment model}) gives a scalar per encoder state -- e.g.\ dot product of decoder and encoder hidden states.
		\item \textbf{Softmax the scores}: pass scores through softmax so they sum to 1 -- the \textbf{attention distribution}.
		\item \textbf{Multiply}: scale each encoder hidden state by its softmaxed score $\Rightarrow$ \textbf{alignment vectors}. This is where alignment happens (\emph{which token matters at this decoding step?}).
		\item \textbf{Sum}: add the alignment vectors $\Rightarrow$ the \textbf{context vector} (aggregated information).
		\item \textbf{Feed to decoder}: combine context vector with the decoder state -- e.g.\ Bahdanau et al.\ (2015) \emph{concatenate} hidden state and context vector. The new decoder state then drives the \emph{next} context vector.
	\end{enumerate}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Wrap-up.} Plain \textbf{Seq2Seq}: encoder--decoder with 2 RNNs; \emph{only} the last encoder state $h_t$ is passed to the decoder. \textbf{Seq2Seq + attention}: \emph{all} encoder states $(h_0,\dots,h_t)$ are available; an attention layer exploits them and the inputs $(x_0,\dots,x_t)$ to refine generation of each next token. Attention = an interface between encoder and decoder giving the decoder information from all (or parts of) the encoder hidden states.
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Transformers}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		A \textbf{Transformer} is a deep-learning architecture for sequential data that processes it \emph{without ordered processing}, relying entirely on the \textbf{attention mechanism}.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Like RNNs}: handle sequential data -- natural language, time series, etc.\ (and images, since pixels are also a sequence -- but first applications were NLP).
		\item \textbf{Unlike RNNs}: no need to process data \emph{in order} (e.g.\ not start-of-sentence before end).
		\item Context/meaning of a token is captured by \textbf{attention}, which weights the influence of different parts of the input.
		\item Order-independence $\Rightarrow$ \textbf{much more parallelisation} than RNNs.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		The \textbf{Transformer} replaces recurrence with \textbf{self-attention}, so all positions are processed in parallel. Key parts: multi-head attention, positional encodings, feed-forward layers, residual + LayerNorm.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{center}
