	\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
		\toprule
		& \textbf{RNN} & \textbf{Transformer} \\
		\midrule
		Computation & serial & parallel \\
		Mixing layer & recurrent (non-linear) & attention (linear) \\
		Token interaction & via hidden state chain & weighted token combinations \\
		Per-token transform & -- & feed-forward (non-linear) \\
		Flow & input $\to$ hidden states & input $\to$ weighted combos $\to$ output \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{"Parallelisation is all you need".} Better parallelisation $\Rightarrow$ shorter training times $\Rightarrow$ larger training sets $\Rightarrow$ better language models. Transformers are now the \textbf{de facto standard for NLP}, surpassing recurrent LSTM-based architectures.
	\end{importantbox}

	\textbf{High-level architecture}: an \textbf{encoding} part + a \textbf{decoding} part + a connection between them.
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Similar in shape to an \textbf{auto-encoder} or \textbf{Seq2Seq} -- but \emph{internally} different from RNN-based Seq2Seq.
		\item In \textbf{pre-trained models}, the encoder or decoder sub-parts can be used \emph{alone}.
		\item Encoding part = a \textbf{stack of $n$ encoder modules}; decoding part = a \textbf{stack of $n$ decoder modules}.
		\item All encoders share the \emph{same structure}; all decoders share the \emph{same structure}.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{No weight sharing} between modules: same structure but \emph{different weights}. The \emph{top} encoder's output is fed to \emph{every} decoder module.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=10mm, font=\small, >=stealth,
		blk/.style={draw, rounded corners, minimum width=22mm, minimum height=12mm}]
		\node[blk, fill=green!12] (enc) {ENCODERS};
		\node[blk, fill=red!10, right=14mm of enc] (dec) {DECODERS};
		\node[below=6mm of enc] (in) {INPUT: \emph{Je suis étudiant}};
		\node[above=6mm of dec] (out) {OUTPUT: \emph{I am a student}};
		\draw[->, thick] (enc)--(dec);
		\draw[->] (in)--(enc);
		\draw[->] (dec)--(out);
	\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}
	
	\subsubsection*{Typical architecture}

	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.58\linewidth}
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
			\item \textbf{Sub-word tokens}: use frequent sub-words as tokens instead of whole words.
			\item \textbf{Positional encoding}: add a vector to the embedding that encodes the token's position (attention is otherwise permutation-invariant).
			\item \textbf{Multi-head attention}: compute several $\mathrm{Attention}(Q,K,V)$ functions in parallel and \emph{concatenate} the results.
			\item \textbf{Self-attention}: each token attends to all tokens in the same sequence.
			\item \textbf{Cross-attention} (decoder): $Q$ from output tokens, $K,V$ from input tokens.
			\item \textbf{Causal / masked self-attention} (decoder): attend only to \emph{previous} output tokens.
			\item \textbf{Residual connections} around self-attention and the feed-forward layer.
			\item \textbf{Normalization} layers (e.g.\ \texttt{Add \& Norm}).
			\item \textbf{Encoder-only} models (e.g.\ BERT) and \textbf{decoder-only} models (e.g.\ GPT).
		\end{itemize}
	\end{minipage}\hfill
	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.38\linewidth}
		\centering
		\includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{images_summary/transformer_architecture.png}
	\end{minipage}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{Scaled dot-product attention:}
		\[
		\mathrm{Attention}(Q,K,V) = \mathrm{softmax}\!\left(\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{d_k}}\right) V
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Q, K, V}: queries, keys, values.
		\item Queries and keys determine which other tokens are relevant; their weights \textbf{sum to 1} (softmax).
		\item New token embedding = \textbf{weighted sum over all token values} ($V$) by the attention weights.
		\item $\sqrt{d_k}$ scaling keeps softmax gradients stable.
		\item \textbf{Complexity $O(n^2)$} in the number of tokens $n$ (every token attends to every token).
	\end{itemize}

	\subsubsection*{Encoder vs decoder module (inner structure)}

	The encoder and decoder modules have a \textbf{different inner structure} (feed-forward = a "simple" dense layer):
	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
		\toprule
		\textbf{Encoder module} & \textbf{Decoder module} \\
		\midrule
		Self-Attention & Self-Attention (\emph{masked}) \\
		-- & Encoder--Decoder Attention \\
		Feed Forward & Feed Forward \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{"Self"}-attention: computed looking \emph{only} at the input sequence -- no data from the decoder.
		\item \textbf{Encoder--Decoder attention}: similar to the attention used in Seq2Seq RNN architectures.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsubsection*{Self-attention layer}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		A \textbf{self-attention layer} implements an attention mechanism by looking at \textbf{adjacent inputs} to produce an \emph{enhanced} representation of the current input.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Like} Seq2Seq attention -- same \emph{objective}.
		\item \textbf{Unlike} Seq2Seq attention -- it looks only at adjacent inputs (the same sequence), not inputs from other parts of the model. Hence \textbf{"self"}.
		\item Implementations may vary.
	\end{itemize}

	\subsubsection*{Word paths through the encoder}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item The first encoder receives the \textbf{word embeddings}; each word then flows through its \emph{own path}.
		\item \textbf{Self-attention} produces an "enhanced" $z_i$ of word $x_i$ at position $i$, accounting for context.
	\end{itemize}
	\begin{formulabox}
		\[
		z_i = f_{sa}(x_1,\dots,x_i,\dots,x_S) \qquad r_i = f_{ff}(z_i)
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Self-attention $f_{sa}$ has \textbf{dependencies} across paths -- $z_i$ depends on \emph{all} $S$ words.
		\item Feed-forward $f_{ff}$ has \textbf{no dependencies} -- $r_i$ depends only on $z_i$ (so paths run in parallel here).
	\end{itemize}

	\subsubsection*{Key--Value--Query computation}

	\textbf{Underlying idea}: project each input vector into \emph{three} spaces for information retrieval.
	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
		\toprule
		\textbf{Query} $q$ & which features around the current token to look for (\emph{"any adjective around?"}) \\
		\textbf{Key} $k$ & tokens projected to align with queries -- like dictionary keys (\emph{"I'm an adjective"}) \\
		\textbf{Value} $v$ & the feature values to retrieve (\emph{"bake in these concepts"}) \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}
	\begin{formulabox}
		\[
		q_i = x_i W^Q \qquad k_i = x_i W^K \qquad v_i = x_i W^V
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $W^Q, W^K, W^V$: matrices \textbf{learned during training} -- like three \textbf{Dense layers without bias}.
		\item Weights are \textbf{shared across all inputs} (a bit like a Conv filter with stride = input vector dim).
		\item In practice \textbf{more than one projection} (multiple queries) -- analogous to multiple filters in CNNs.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
		\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{images_summary/self_attention_example.png}
	\end{center}
	Worked example: each query scores against every key $\to$ multiply each value by its score $\to$ sum (\emph{addition}) gives that position's output.

	\textbf{Computation steps:}
	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
		\item Map all inputs to key--value--query vectors (typically 3 Dense layers).
		\item For a given input:
		\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, label=\arabic*.]
			\item Compute attention scores between this input and all adjacent inputs; \textbf{normalize with softmax}.
			\item Multiply the value vectors by the scores -- \emph{amplifies} relevant inputs, \emph{de-amplifies} irrelevant ones.
			\item Add the results to get this input's output.
		\end{enumerate}
	\end{enumerate}

	\subsection{Exam Tips}
	
	\begin{examtipbox}
		\textbf{Likely questions \& pitfalls:}
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
			\item \emph{"Why $\sqrt{d_k}$?"} -- large dot products push softmax into saturated regions $\Rightarrow$ vanishing gradients; scaling keeps them stable. (\textbf{apply}, not derive.)
			\item \emph{"Why are Transformers faster to train than RNNs?"} -- no ordered processing $\Rightarrow$ the sequence is processed in parallel (RNNs are inherently serial).
			\item Know the \textbf{three attention types}: encoder self-attention, \emph{masked} decoder self-attention, encoder--decoder \emph{cross}-attention.
			\item \emph{"Why positional encoding?"} -- self-attention is \textbf{permutation-invariant}; without it word order is lost.
			\item \textbf{Complexity}: self-attention is $O(n^2)$ in sequence length $n$ -- the motivation for efficient/long-context variants.
			\item Common mistake: thinking $Q,K,V$ are different inputs -- they are \emph{three learned projections of the same input} (in self-attention).
			\item Within a module only \textbf{self-attention couples tokens}; the feed-forward layer acts \emph{per-token} (independent paths).
			\item \emph{"Compare BERT vs GPT"} -- encoder-only (bidirectional) vs decoder-only (causal/autoregressive).
			\item Don't confuse \textbf{LayerNorm} (per-token, used in Transformers) with BatchNorm.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{examtipbox}
	
	% ==================================================
	\section{Generative Models -- VAE, GAN, Diffusion}
	% ==================================================
	
	\subsection{Variational Autoencoder (VAE)}

	\textbf{Core idea.}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{Goal:} generate new samples that follow the unknown \textbf{data distribution} $x\sim q$. Do it via a \textbf{latent variable model}: sample a latent $z$ from an easy prior, then decode it to a data point.
		\[
		z \sim p_{\text{prior}}(z), \qquad x \sim p_\theta(x|z)
		\]
		$p_{\text{prior}}(z)$: fixed easy prior (usually $\mathcal{N}(0,I)$). \quad $p_\theta(x|z)$: \textbf{decoder}, maps latent $\to$ data.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{Ideal objective = maximum likelihood (MLE).} Pick $\theta$ so the model puts probability mass where real data lives:
		\[
		\hat\theta = \arg\max_\theta\;
		\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim q}\big[\log p_\theta(x)\big]}_{\text{avg.\ log-lik.\ of real data}},
		\qquad
		p_\theta(x) = \mathbb{E}_{z\sim p_{\text{prior}}}\big[p_\theta(x|z)\big]
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $\mathbb{E}_{x\sim q}[\cdot]$: sample a \emph{real} point from data dist.\ $q$, evaluate $\log p_\theta$ there. With a dataset $\{x^{(i)}\}$ this is just the Monte-Carlo / empirical MLE average $\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_i \log p_\theta(x^{(i)})$.
		\item \textbf{Two distributions over $x$ -- keep them apart:} \;$q(x)$ = true data dist.\ (can \emph{sample}, no formula); \;$p_\theta(x)$ = model dist.\ (can \emph{evaluate}, controlled by $\theta$).
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		The marginal $p_\theta(x)=\int p_\theta(x|z)\,p_{\text{prior}}(z)\,dz$ is \textbf{intractable} (integral over all $z$). \\
		\textbf{Fix:} learn an \textbf{encoder} $q_\phi(z|x)$ that maps data into the latent space so it matches the prior, then chain it with the decoder $p_\theta(x|z)$ -- and optimize the tractable \textbf{ELBO} instead of $\log p_\theta(x)$ directly.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize, >=Stealth,
		blk/.style={draw, rounded corners=1pt, inner sep=2.5pt, font=\scriptsize},
		enc/.style={->, thick, orange!85!black},
		dec/.style={->, thick, blue!70},
		gen/.style={->, dotted, thick, gray}]
		% --- z-space (latent) circle: distribution p_theta(z) ---
		\node[circle, draw, fill=gray!25, minimum size=13mm] (zs) at (0,3.0) {};
		\fill[gray!50] ($(zs)+(-1.3mm,1.4mm)$) ellipse (1.7mm and 1.1mm);
		\node[circle, fill=orange, draw=orange!50!black, inner sep=1.1pt] (zi) at ($(zs)+(1.4mm,-1.2mm)$) {};
		\node[font=\tiny] at ($(zi)+(2.4mm,-0.4mm)$) {$z$};
		\node[font=\tiny\bfseries, right=1mm of zs.east] {z-space};
		% --- prior ---
		\node[blk] (prior) at (0,4.15) {Prior $p_\theta(z)$};
		% --- encoder / decoder ---
		\node[blk] (enc) at (-2.05,1.75) {Encoder $q_\phi(z|x)$};
		\node[blk] (dec) at (2.05,1.75) {Decoder $p_\theta(x|z)$};
		% --- x-space: data distribution q(x) as an irregular manifold ---
		\draw[fill=blue!10, draw=blue!45, thick] plot[smooth cycle, tension=0.85] coordinates
			{(-0.85,1.45) (-0.45,1.62) (-0.05,1.5) (0.42,1.68) (0.86,1.46)
			 (0.55,1.2) (0.15,1.34) (-0.32,1.18) (-0.72,1.3)};
		\node[circle, fill=orange, draw=orange!50!black, inner sep=1.1pt] (xi) at (0.42,1.68) {};
		\node[font=\tiny] at ($(xi)+(1.6mm,1.5mm)$) {$x$};
		\node[font=\tiny\bfseries] at (1.6,1.25) {x-space};
		% --- dataset ---
		\node[blk] (data) at (0,0.15) {Dataset $\mathbf{D}$};
		% --- generative side (dotted, gray) ---
		\draw[gen] (prior) -- (zs);
		\draw[gen] (data) -- (0,1.05);
		% --- encode a single point: x -> encoder -> z ---
		\draw[enc] (xi) to[bend left=18] (enc.south);
		\draw[enc] (enc.north) to[bend left=18] (zi);
		\node[font=\tiny, orange!85!black] at (-1.55,2.85) {encode};
		% --- decode it back: z -> decoder -> x ---
		\draw[dec] (zi) to[bend left=18] (dec.north);
		\draw[dec] (dec.south) to[bend left=18] (xi);
		\node[font=\tiny, blue!70] at (1.55,2.85) {decode};
	\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}
	{\footnotesize Blue blob = data distribution $q(x)$ in \textbf{x-space} (samples = Dataset $\mathbf{D}$). A point $x$ is \textcolor{orange!85!black}{\textbf{encoded}} to a latent $z$ in \textbf{z-space} (shaped to match the prior $p_\theta(z)$), then \textcolor{blue!70}{\textbf{decoded}} back to reconstruct $x$. \emph{(Kingma \& Welling, 2019)}}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		A \textbf{VAE} learns a probabilistic latent space: the encoder $q_\phi(z|x)$ outputs a distribution, the decoder $p_\theta(x|z)$ reconstructs $x$ from sampled $z$. Trained by maximizing the \textbf{ELBO}.
	\end{definitionbox}
	
	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{Closed form} (Gaussian $q_\phi$, $\mathcal{N}(0,I)$ prior):
		\[
		\mathcal{L}_{\text{VAE}}(x,\hat x) =
		\underbrace{\|\hat x - x\|^2}_{\text{reconstruction } L_{rec}}
		+ \underbrace{\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_i\big(\exp(\mathrm{logvar}_i(x)) + \mu_i(x)^2 - 1 - \mathrm{logvar}_i(x)\big)}_{D_{KL}(q_\phi(z|x)\,\|\,p_{\text{prior}}(z))}
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $\|\hat x - x\|^2$: \textbf{reconstruction loss} (squared error, as in a plain AE); $\hat x$ depends on encoder \emph{and} decoder params $\phi,\theta$.
		\item $\mu_i(x),\mathrm{logvar}_i(x)$: encoder outputs (depend on $\phi$). Using $\mathrm{logvar}$ and $\sigma_i = \exp(\mathrm{logvar}_i/2)$ guarantees a positive std.\ dev.
		\item The sum is the closed-form KL between $\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^2)$ and $\mathcal{N}(0,I)$ (with $\sigma_i^2=\exp(\mathrm{logvar}_i)$).
	\end{itemize}
	
	The \textbf{reparameterization trick} ($z = \mu + \sigma \odot \epsilon$, $\epsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)$) makes sampling differentiable.

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth, font=\small,
		bar/.style={draw, minimum width=7mm, minimum height=24mm, rounded corners=1pt},
		ms/.style={draw, fill=green!25, minimum width=9mm, minimum height=6mm, font=\bfseries},
		lat/.style={draw, fill=red!55, text=white, minimum width=7mm, minimum height=10mm, font=\bfseries}]

		% input / output bars
		\node[bar, fill=green!45] (x) {$\mathbf{x}$};
		\node[above=1mm of x, font=\scriptsize\bfseries, fill=gray!20, inner sep=2pt] {Input};

		% encoder mean/std
		\node[ms, right=14mm of x, yshift=7mm]  (mu)    {$\boldsymbol{\mu}$};
		\node[ms, right=14mm of x, yshift=-7mm] (sigma) {$\boldsymbol{\sigma}$};
		\node[left=0.5mm of mu, font=\scriptsize] {Mean};
		\node[left=0.5mm of sigma, font=\scriptsize] {Std.\ dev};
		\node[above=5mm of mu, font=\scriptsize\bfseries, green!50!black, align=center]
			(enc) {Probabilistic Encoder\\[-1pt] $q_\phi(\mathbf{z}|\mathbf{x})$};

		% latent
		\node[lat, right=20mm of mu, yshift=-7mm] (z) {$\mathbf{z}$};
		\node[above=2mm of z, font=\scriptsize\bfseries, red!70!black, align=center] {Sampled\\[-1pt] latent vector};

		% decoder (trapezium opening to the right)
		\node[draw, fill=blue!55, text=white, trapezium, trapezium left angle=110, trapezium right angle=70,
			shape border rotate=270, minimum width=20mm, minimum height=18mm, right=16mm of z,
			align=center, font=\scriptsize\bfseries] (dec) {Probabilistic\\ Decoder\\ $p_\theta(\mathbf{x}|\mathbf{z})$};

		% reconstructed bar
		\node[bar, fill=blue!35, right=14mm of dec] (xp) {$\mathbf{x'}$};
		\node[above=1mm of xp, font=\scriptsize\bfseries, fill=gray!20, inner sep=2pt] {Reconstructed input};

		% arrows
		\draw[->] (x.east) -- (mu.west);
		\draw[->] (x.east) -- (sigma.west);
		\draw[->] (mu.east)    -- ([yshift=4mm]z.west);
		\draw[->] (sigma.east) -- ([yshift=-4mm]z.west);
		\draw[->] (z) -- (dec);
		\draw[->] (dec) -- (xp);

		% top identical note
		\node[above=11mm of dec, font=\scriptsize, align=center]
			{Ideally they are identical.\\[1pt] $\mathbf{x}\approx\mathbf{x'}$};

		% bottom captions
		\node[below=12mm of sigma, font=\scriptsize] {$\mathbf{z}=\boldsymbol{\mu}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}\odot\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\;\; \boldsymbol{\epsilon}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)$};
		\node[below=2mm of z, font=\scriptsize, align=center, text width=42mm]
			{A compressed low-dimensional representation of the input.};
	\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}

	\subsection{Kullback--Leibler (KL) Divergence}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		The \textbf{KL divergence} measures the discrepancy between two probability densities $p(\cdot),q(\cdot)$ on $X$ -- ``how far'' $q$ is from $p$. It is the regularizer in the VAE ELBO.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\[
		D_{KL}(p\,\|\,q) = \int_X p(x)\,\log\frac{p(x)}{q(x)}\,dx
		= \mathbb{E}_{x\sim p}\!\left[\log\frac{p(x)}{q(x)}\right]
		\]
		\[
		D_{KL}(p\|q) =
		\underbrace{-\!\int_X p(x)\log q(x)\,dx}_{\text{cross-entropy } H(p,q)}
		-\underbrace{\Big(\!-\!\int_X p(x)\log p(x)\,dx\Big)}_{\text{entropy } H(p)}
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $p,q$: densities on $X$. \quad The expectation is taken under $p$ (the \textbf{first} argument).
	\end{itemize}

	\textbf{Properties:}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Not symmetric:} $D_{KL}(p\|q)\neq D_{KL}(q\|p)$ $\Rightarrow$ \emph{not} a distance metric (no triangle inequality).
		\item \textbf{Non-negative:} $D_{KL}(p\|q)\ge 0$, with equality iff $p=q$. Easy to prove since $-\log$ is \textbf{convex} (apply Jensen's inequality).
	\end{itemize}
	
	\begin{importantbox}
		$H(p)$ does not depend on $q$, so \textbf{minimizing $D_{KL}(p\|q)$ over $q$ $=$ minimizing the cross-entropy $H(p,q)$} -- this is why MLE is equivalent to minimizing cross-entropy. In the VAE, the term $D_{KL}\big(q_\phi(z|x)\,\|\,p(z)\big)$ pulls the encoder toward the prior.
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
			\item ``Why is KL \emph{not} a distance?'' $\to$ not symmetric, no triangle inequality.
			\item \textbf{Forward vs reverse KL:} $D_{KL}(p\|q)$ is mass-covering (mean-seeking); $D_{KL}(q\|p)$ is mode-seeking. VAEs minimize the \emph{reverse} form $D_{KL}(q_\phi\|p)$.
			\item Remember: $\text{KL} = \text{cross-entropy} - \text{entropy}$.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Generative Adversarial Network (GAN)}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		A \textbf{GAN} trains two networks in an \textbf{adversarial game}: a \textbf{generator} $G_\phi$ that maps random noise $z$ to fake samples, and a \textbf{discriminator} $D_\theta$ that scores samples as real or fake. They are trained \emph{against} each other until fakes are indistinguishable from real data.
	\end{definitionbox}

	% --- Architecture: adversarial game ---
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzpicture}[
			font=\footnotesize, >={Stealth[round]}, node distance=6mm,
			box/.style={rounded corners=1pt, draw, minimum height=8mm, minimum width=20mm, align=center, text=white, font=\footnotesize\bfseries},
			lbl/.style={align=center, font=\scriptsize}]

			% nodes
			\node[box, fill=gray!55] (noise) at (0,0) {Random\\Noise $z$};
			\node[box, fill=blue!70]  (gen)   at (0,-2) {Generator\\$G_\phi$};
			\node[box, fill=green!55!black] (fake) at (3.6,-2) {Fake\\Data $G(z)$};
			\node[box, fill=yellow!75!black, text=black] (real) at (3.6,0) {Real\\Data $x$};
			\node[box, fill=magenta!70] (disc) at (7.4,-1) {Discriminator\\$D_\theta$};
			\node[lbl] (score) at (10.3,-1) {score\\real / fake};

			% edges
			\draw[->] (noise) -- (gen);
			\draw[->] (gen)   -- (fake);
			\draw[->] (real)  -- (disc);
			\draw[->] (fake)  -- (disc);
			\draw[->] (disc)  -- (score);

			% captions
			\node[lbl, blue!70!black] at (0,-3.1) {learns to fool $D$};
			\node[lbl, magenta!60!black] at (7.4,-2.2) {learns to tell real from fake};
			\node[font=\scriptsize] at (7.4,0.9) {$D_\theta(x)=\begin{cases}1 & x:\text{real}\\ 0 & x:\text{gen}\end{cases}$};
		\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{Minimax (value) objective -- the ``adversarial game'':}
		\[
		\min_G \max_D \; V(D,G) =
		\mathbb{E}_{x\sim p_{\text{data}}}[\log D(x)] + \mathbb{E}_{z\sim p_z}[\log(1 - D(G(z)))]
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $D$ \textbf{maximises} $V$: push $D(x)\to 1$ for real, $D(G(z))\to 0$ for fake.
		\item $G$ \textbf{minimises} $V$: push $D(G(z))\to 1$ so fakes look real.
		\item $p_{\text{data}}$: true data distribution \quad $p_z$: prior noise (e.g.\ $\mathcal{N}(0,I)$).
	\end{itemize}

	\medskip
	\textbf{In practice the game is split into two binary cross-entropy losses}, optimised in alternating steps (real $\to$ label $1$, generated $\to$ label $0$).

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{Discriminator loss} (generator $G_\phi$ fixed):
		\[
		L_{\text{disc}}(D_\theta) = -\tfrac{1}{2}\Big(
		\mathbb{E}_{x\sim p_{\text{real}}}[\log D_\theta(x)] +
		\mathbb{E}_{z\sim p_{\text{latent}}}[\log(1 - D_\theta(G_\phi(z)))]\Big)
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Small} when $D_\theta(\cdot)$ is close to $1$ for real $x$ and close to $0$ for generated $x$; \textbf{large} the other way around.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{Generator loss} (discriminator $D_\theta$ fixed) -- the \emph{non-saturating} form:
		\[
		L_{\text{gen}}(G_\phi) = -\,\mathbb{E}_{z\sim p_{\text{latent}}}[\log(D_\theta(G_\phi(z)))]
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Small} when the discriminator is fooled, $D_\theta(x_{\text{gen}})\approx 1$; \textbf{large} when $D$ correctly recognises generated samples.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		$L_{\text{gen}}$ has the \textbf{same optima} as just using the negative discriminator loss as target, i.e.\ $L_{\text{gen}} = -L_{\text{disc}}$ (the original minimax game) -- but the $-\log D(G(z))$ form \textbf{behaves better in early training}: it gives $G$ strong gradients while $D$ still easily rejects fakes, avoiding the \textbf{vanishing-gradient} problem of $\log(1-D(G(z)))$.
	\end{importantbox}

	\medskip
	\textbf{Training loop} (alternating gradient steps -- $D$ then $G$):
	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Pick a mini-batch} of samples:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item $(z^{(1)},\dots,z^{(m)})$ from the noise prior $p_{\text{latent}}(z)$.
			\item $(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(m)})$ from the data distribution $p_{\text{data}}(x)$.
		\end{itemize}
		\item \textbf{Update the discriminator} by gradient descent on $L_{\text{disc}}$ (generator from the previous step held fixed):
		\[ \nabla_\theta L_{\text{disc}}(D_\theta) \]
		\item \textbf{Update the generator} by gradient descent on $L_{\text{gen}}$ (discriminator from step 2 held fixed):
		\[ \nabla_\phi L_{\text{gen}}(G_\phi) \]
	\end{enumerate}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{GANs are hard to train.} Two main reasons:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item \textbf{Search for a Nash equilibrium} ($\approx$ saddle point): $G$ and $D$ have \emph{opposing} targets, so we need a good \emph{compromise} -- neither network should grow too strong relative to the other.
			\item \textbf{No convergence guarantee:} unlike standard loss minimisation, there is \emph{no guarantee the loss decreases} -- training can \textbf{oscillate} without converging.
			\item \textbf{Mode collapse:} the generator fails to represent the full \emph{variety} of the original data, producing only a few distinct samples.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{importantbox}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		\textbf{Likely GAN questions:}
		\begin{itemize}
			\item \emph{``Write/explain the minimax objective.''} Know who maximises vs minimises: $D$ maximises ($D(x)\to1$, $D(G(z))\to0$), $G$ minimises. Common trap: swapping the two.
			\item \emph{``Why use the non-saturating generator loss $-\log D(G(z))$ instead of $\log(1-D(G(z)))$?''} $\to$ same optima, but stronger gradients for $G$ early on (avoids vanishing gradients when $D$ easily rejects fakes).
			\item \emph{``What is mode collapse / why are GANs hard to train?''} $\to$ no guaranteed loss decrease, oscillations, search for a Nash equilibrium (saddle point), neither net should dominate.
			\item \emph{``Describe one training iteration.''} $\to$ alternating: update $D$ ($\nabla_\theta L_{\text{disc}}$) then $G$ ($\nabla_\phi L_{\text{gen}}$) on a mini-batch of real + noise samples.
			\item \emph{Compare/contrast with VAE} $\to$ GAN: sharp samples, unstable, no explicit likelihood; VAE: blurry but stable, principled latent space.
		\end{itemize}
		\textbf{Common mistakes:} confusing $G$/$D$ roles or labels (real$=1$, fake$=0$); claiming the loss always decreases; forgetting the two updates are \emph{alternating} (not simultaneous).
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Diffusion Models}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		A \textbf{diffusion model} learns to generate data by reversing a gradual noising process. A fixed \textbf{forward process} $q$ destroys the data by adding small amounts of Gaussian noise over $T$ steps until it becomes pure noise; a learned \textbf{reverse process} $p_\theta$ denoises step by step to turn noise back into a sample. State of the art for image generation (e.g.\ Stable Diffusion).
	\end{definitionbox}

	\textbf{(Forward) Diffusion Process} -- fixed, no learning; gradually corrupts $x_0\to x_T$:


	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{One forward (noising) step} -- reparameterized sampling:
		\[
			x_t = \sqrt{1-\beta_t}\,x_{t-1} + \sqrt{\beta_t}\,\epsilon,
			\qquad \epsilon \sim \mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{1}_d)
		\]
		equivalently a Gaussian transition kernel
		\[
			q(x_t\mid x_{t-1}) = \mathcal{N}\!\big(x_t;\ \sqrt{1-\beta_t}\,x_{t-1},\ \beta_t\mathbf{1}_d\big)
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $x_t$: noisy sample at step $t$; \quad $x_0$: clean data, $x_T$: (almost) pure noise.
		\item $\beta_t$: \textbf{variance schedule} -- amount of noise added at step $t$ (small, increasing).
		\item $\epsilon$: standard Gaussian noise; $\mathbf{1}_d$: $d$-dim identity covariance.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		Choose \textbf{small} $\beta_t$ and \textbf{sufficiently many steps} $T$ ($T\approx 1000$) so $x_T$ ends up as \textbf{pure noise}. The forward process has \emph{no parameters}; the goal is to \textbf{learn to revert} it, i.e.\ to estimate the unknown reverse step $q(x_{t-1}\mid x_t)$.
	\end{importantbox}

	\textbf{Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DDPM)} -- a neural net learns the reverse:

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=14mm, font=\small, >={Stealth[round]},
		st/.style={circle, draw, minimum size=9mm, inner sep=0pt},
		noise/.style={st, fill=gray!55},
		mid/.style={st, fill=gray!30},
		clean/.style={st, fill=white}]
		% chain left (noise) -> right (data)
		\node[noise] (xT) {$x_T$};
		\node[right=8mm of xT] (dotsL) {$\cdots$};
		\node[mid, right=8mm of dotsL] (xt) {$x_t$};
		\node[mid, right=of xt] (xt1) {$x_{t-1}$};
		\node[right=8mm of xt1] (dotsR) {$\cdots$};
		\node[clean, right=8mm of dotsR] (x0) {$x_0$};
		% priors
		\node[font=\scriptsize, above=1mm of xT] {$p_\theta(x_T)$};
		\node[font=\scriptsize, above=1mm of x0] {$p_\theta(x_0)$};
		% reverse (learned) chain, point right toward data
		\draw[->] (xT) -- (dotsL);
		\draw[->] (dotsL) -- (xt);
		\draw[->, blue, thick] (xt) -- node[above=2mm, font=\scriptsize, blue]{$p_\theta(x_{t-1}\mid x_t)$} (xt1);
		\draw[->] (xt1) -- (dotsR);
		\draw[->] (dotsR) -- (x0);
		% forward (noising) step below, point left toward noise
		\draw[->, gray] (xt1.south) to[bend left=15]
			node[below, font=\scriptsize]{$q(x_t\mid x_{t-1})$} (xt.south);
		% labels
		\node[blue, font=\scriptsize\bfseries, align=center, above=12mm of xt] (rev)
			{Reverse Diffusion: De-Noising \emph{(learned)} $\Rightarrow$};
		\node[green!50!black, font=\scriptsize\bfseries, align=center, below=2mm of rev] (nn) {Neural\\Network};
		\draw[->, green!50!black] (nn.south) to[bend right=10] ($(xt)!0.2!(xt1)+(0,0.65)$);
		\node[gray, font=\scriptsize\bfseries, align=center, below=8mm of xt]
			{$\Leftarrow$ Forward Diffusion: Adding Noise (per schedule)};
	\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Reverse} (right-ward, blue): the \textbf{neural network} $p_\theta(x_{t-1}\mid x_t)$ learns to denoise -- this is what is trained.
		\item \textbf{Forward} (left-ward, gray): fixed noising $q(x_t\mid x_{t-1})$ provides the training targets.
		\item In practice the net predicts the \textbf{noise} $\epsilon$ added at each step; loss is a simple $\|\epsilon - \epsilon_\theta(x_t,t)\|^2$ regression.
	\end{itemize}

	\medskip
	\textbf{Optimisation Target.} We want maximum likelihood, i.e.\ minimise the \textbf{negative log-likelihood} of the training samples -- but this is \emph{not tractable}:
	\begin{formulabox}
		\[
			-\sum_{x_0}\log\big(p_\theta(x_0)\big)
			\qquad(\text{\small$x_0$: training samples})
		\]
	\end{formulabox}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Fix:} bound it by a \textbf{variational upper bound} $L_\theta$ (KL term $\ge 0$, so it is a valid upper bound):
	\end{itemize}
	\begin{formulabox}
		\[
			-\log\big(p_\theta(x_0)\big) \;\le\;
			-\log\big(p_\theta(x_0)\big) + D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\big(q(x_{1:T}\mid x_0)\,\|\,p_\theta(x_{1:T}\mid x_0)\big)
			\;=:\; L_\theta
		\]
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $x_{1:T}$: the whole noised trajectory; $q(x_{1:T}|x_0)$: fixed forward process; $p_\theta(x_{1:T}|x_0)$: learned reverse.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		After \emph{``some maths''} the bound $L_\theta$ simplifies to a plain \textbf{noise-prediction (denoising) regression} -- this is the loss actually used in DDPM:
		\[
			L_{\text{simple}}(\theta) =
			\mathbb{E}\Big[\,\big\|\,\epsilon - \epsilon_\theta\big(\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0 + \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\epsilon,\ t\big)\,\big\|^2\,\Big]
		\]
	\end{importantbox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $\epsilon_\theta$: \textbf{learnable function} (the neural network) -- predicts the noise.
		\item $\epsilon \sim \mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{1}_d)$: the true Gaussian noise to be recovered.
		\item $\bar\alpha_t = \prod_{s\le t}(1-\beta_s)$: \textbf{noise schedule} -- lets us jump to step $t$ in \emph{one} shot ($x_t=\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0+\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\epsilon$).
		\item $x_0$: \textbf{sample from the training set}; \quad $t$: \textbf{sampled uniformly} from $\{1,\dots,T\}$.
	\end{itemize}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Training:} gradient descent on $L_{\text{simple}}(\theta)$ -- sample $x_0$, $t$, $\epsilon$; noise $x_0\to x_t$; have the net predict $\epsilon$; minimise squared error.
	\end{itemize}

	\medskip
	\textbf{Training vs.\ Sampling algorithms.}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{Algorithm 1 -- Training} (each iteration is \emph{one single step}, no loop over $t$):
		\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em, itemsep=1pt]
			\item \textbf{repeat}
			\item \quad $x_0 \sim q(x_0)$ \hfill {\scriptsize(draw a training sample)}
			\item \quad $t \sim \mathrm{Uniform}(\{1,\dots,T\})$ \hfill {\scriptsize(random step)}
			\item \quad $\epsilon \sim \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})$
			\item \quad gradient descent step on $\big\|\,\epsilon - \epsilon_\theta(\underbrace{\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0 + \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\epsilon}_{=\,x_t},\ t)\,\big\|^2$
			\item \textbf{until} converged
		\end{enumerate}
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $t$ is fed to the net as a \textbf{sufficiently powerful encoding of time} (e.g.\ sinusoidal positional embedding) so one network handles all steps.
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{formulabox}
		\textbf{Algorithm 2 -- Sampling} (generate from noise, $T$ sequential denoising steps):
		\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em, itemsep=1pt]
			\item $x_T \sim \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})$ \hfill {\scriptsize(start from pure noise)}
			\item \textbf{for} $t = T,\dots,1$ \textbf{do}
			\item \quad $z \sim \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})$ \; (and $z=\mathbf{0}$ at $t=1$)
			\item \quad $x_{t-1} = \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_t}}\Big(x_t - \dfrac{1-\alpha_t}{\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}}\,\epsilon_\theta(x_t,t)\Big) + \sigma_t\, z$
			\item \textbf{end for}
			\item \textbf{return} $x_0$
		\end{enumerate}
	\end{formulabox}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item $\alpha_t = 1-\beta_t$, \; $\bar\alpha_t = \prod_{s\le t}\alpha_s$, \; $\sigma_t$: per-step noise scale (e.g.\ $\sqrt{\beta_t}$).
		\item Sampling needs \textbf{$T\approx1000$ sequential steps} (each a full net pass) $\Rightarrow$ \textbf{slow} -- the main drawback vs.\ GAN/VAE (one forward pass).
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{importantbox}
		\textbf{Asymmetry to remember:} \emph{training} is \textbf{one step at a time} (sample a random $t$, predict its noise -- cheap); \emph{sampling} is the \textbf{full $T$-step chain} run sequentially -- this is why diffusion is high-quality but slow to sample.
	\end{importantbox}

	\subsection{Exam Tips}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		\textbf{Likely diffusion questions:}
		\begin{itemize}
			\item \emph{``Describe the forward vs reverse process.''} $\to$ forward $q$ is \textbf{fixed} (adds Gaussian noise per schedule $\beta_t$), reverse $p_\theta$ is \textbf{learned} (denoises). Don't swap which one is learned.
			\item \emph{``Write the forward step.''} $\to$ $x_t=\sqrt{1-\beta_t}\,x_{t-1}+\sqrt{\beta_t}\,\epsilon$, i.e.\ $q(x_t|x_{t-1})=\mathcal{N}(\sqrt{1-\beta_t}\,x_{t-1},\beta_t\mathbf{1})$.
			\item \emph{``Why many steps / small $\beta_t$?''} $\to$ small per-step changes make each reverse step nearly Gaussian (so a net can model it); $T\approx1000$ to reach pure noise.
			\item \emph{``What does the network predict?''} $\to$ usually the added noise $\epsilon$; trained with an $\ell_2$ noise-prediction loss.
			\item \emph{``Why not just maximise likelihood directly?''} $\to$ NLL $-\sum\log p_\theta(x_0)$ is \textbf{intractable}; bound it by the variational $L_\theta$ (KL $\ge0$), which simplifies to $L_{\text{simple}}$. Know that $\bar\alpha_t$ lets you noise $x_0\to x_t$ in one step, and $t$ is drawn uniformly.
			\item \emph{``Contrast training vs sampling.''} $\to$ training $=$ \textbf{one random step} per update (cheap); sampling $=$ \textbf{$T\approx1000$ sequential} denoising passes (slow). Common trap: thinking training also loops over all $T$ steps.
		\end{itemize}
	\end{examtipbox}
	
	\begin{examtipbox}
		VAE: blurry but stable, principled latent space. GAN: sharp samples, unstable training, mode collapse. Diffusion: high quality, slow sampling (many steps). Remember the reparameterization trick is what lets VAEs backprop through sampling.
	\end{examtipbox}

	\subsection{Wrap-up: GAN vs.\ VAE vs.\ Diffusion}

	\begin{definitionbox}
		\textbf{The generative learning trilemma.} A generative model ideally wants \textbf{(1) high-quality samples}, \textbf{(2) fast sampling}, and \textbf{(3) mode coverage / diversity}. In practice each family achieves only \textbf{two of the three}.
	\end{definitionbox}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small, >={Stealth[round]},
		corner/.style={circle, draw, very thick, minimum size=22mm, align=center, font=\footnotesize\bfseries}]
		% three corners of the triangle
		\node[corner] (HQ)  at (0,3.2)    {High\\Quality\\Samples};
		\node[corner] (FS)  at (-3.2,-1)  {Fast\\Sampling};
		\node[corner] (MC)  at (3.2,-1)   {Mode\\Coverage\\/ Diversity};
		% GAN: HQ + FS (blue), missing diversity
		\draw[blue, dash dot, thick] (HQ) -- (FS);
		% Diffusion: HQ + MC (red), missing speed
		\draw[red, dashed, thick] (HQ) -- (MC);
		% VAE / Normalizing Flows: FS + MC (orange), missing quality
		\draw[orange, densely dashed, thick] (FS) -- (MC);
		% family labels along each edge
		\node[blue, align=center, font=\footnotesize\bfseries] at (-3.4,1.7)
			{Generative\\Adversarial\\Networks};
		\node[red, align=center, font=\footnotesize\bfseries] at (3.5,1.7)
			{Denoising\\Diffusion\\Models};
		\node[orange, align=center, font=\footnotesize\bfseries] at (0,-2.6)
			{Variational Autoencoders,\\Normalizing Flows};
	\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}
	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textcolor{blue}{\textbf{GANs}}: high quality $+$ fast $-$ \emph{weak diversity} (mode collapse).
		\item \textcolor{red}{\textbf{Diffusion}}: high quality $+$ diverse $-$ \emph{slow} ($T\approx1000$ steps).
		\item \textcolor{orange!85!black}{\textbf{VAEs / flows}}: fast $+$ diverse $-$ \emph{lower sample quality} (blurry).
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
		\toprule
		& \textbf{Sample quality} & \textbf{Sampling speed} & \textbf{Diversity} & \textbf{Training} \\
		\midrule
		VAE       & blurry        & fast (1 pass)      & good   & stable, ELBO \\
		GAN       & sharp         & fast (1 pass)      & poor (collapse) & unstable, minimax \\
		Diffusion & sharp/SOTA    & slow ($T$ steps)   & good   & stable, denoising MSE \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
	\end{center}

	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
		\item \textbf{Likelihood:} VAE optimises a likelihood bound (ELBO); diffusion optimises a likelihood bound too ($L_\theta$); GAN has \emph{no explicit likelihood} (implicit, adversarial).
		\item \textbf{Latent space:} VAE has a structured, low-dim latent $z$ (good for interpolation); GAN maps noise $z\to x$; diffusion's ``latent'' is the same dimensionality as $x$ (the noise $x_T$).
	\end{itemize}

	\begin{examtipbox}
		\textbf{Trilemma is an examiner favourite.} Be ready to: \emph{place each family on the triangle} (which 2 of 3 it gets, which it sacrifices), and justify -- \textbf{GAN} sacrifices diversity, \textbf{diffusion} sacrifices speed, \textbf{VAE/flows} sacrifice quality. Common follow-ups: ``why is diffusion slow?'' ($T\!\approx\!1000$ sequential passes), ``why does the GAN lack diversity?'' (mode collapse), ``which has an explicit likelihood?'' (VAE/diffusion bound, \emph{not} GAN).
	\end{examtipbox}
